--- AgentMessage ---
Decision 38 recorded, with your hierarchy as the product model. One maintainability adjustment is important: Operations should reference reusable agent definitions rather than copying each agent’s full configuration. Otherwise the same Researcher duplicated across five Operations will drift into five inconsistent versions.

--- AgentMessage ---
Yes, your model is clear:

```text
Pack
└── Multiple Operations
    └── Multiple Agents
        ├── Skills
        ├── Tools
        └── Connectors
```

The important refinement is to separate reusable definitions from Operation-specific assignments.

## Recommended canonical model

```text
Pack
├── Agent catalog
│   ├── Researcher
│   ├── Citation Manager
│   ├── Programming Tutor
│   └── Data Analyst
│
└── Operations
    ├── Research Paper
    │   ├── Researcher
    │   ├── Citation Manager
    │   └── Writing Coach
    │
    ├── Exam Preparation
    │   ├── Study Planner
    │   ├── Subject Tutor
    │   └── Source Verifier
    │
    └── Programming Assignment
        ├── Programming Tutor
        ├── Developer
        └── Debugger
```

Each reusable agent definition owns its default capabilities:

```text
Agent: Researcher
├── Skills
│   ├── Web Research
│   ├── Literature Review
│   └── Evidence Synthesis
├── Tools
│   ├── Web Search
│   ├── Page Reader
│   ├── PDF Parser
│   └── Artifact Writer
└── Connectors
    ├── Google Drive
    └── Reference Manager
```

An Operation then selects agents and may narrow or customize their capabilities:

```text
Operation: Research Paper
└── Researcher assignment
    ├── enabled skills
    │   ├── Literature Review
    │   └── Evidence Synthesis
    ├── enabled tools
    │   ├── Web Search
    │   └── PDF Parser
    ├── allowed connectors
    │   └── Google Drive
    └── operation instructions
        └── Focus on academic sources
```

## Why the distinction matters

The same Researcher can participate in multiple Operations without duplicating its entire profile:

```text
Researcher
├── Research Paper
├── Data Analysis
├── Presentation Preparation
└── Exam Preparation
```

Each Operation can narrow the Researcher’s behavior without creating a new copy.

This gives us:

- one source of truth for each agent
- reusable agents across Operations
- Operation-specific capability restrictions
- fewer duplicated prompts
- simpler p

320	- Adding Temporal or another orchestration dependency now would increase operational and migration cost before the product shape is validated.

326	- actor and reason
327	- idempotency key
328	- optimistic-concurrency/version check
329	- emitted event
330	- retry and timeout policy
331	- recovery behavior

344	**Question 9: Approval and permission model**
345	
346	How should approval decisions be represented and applied?
347	
348	1. **Per-action approval only:** every risky action pauses and requires a new approval.
349	2. **Reusable grants, recommended:** support one-time approval plus scoped reusable grants for a tool, agent, conversation, or user, with expiry and revocation.
350	3. **Role-based permissions:** administrators assign broad permission roles to users or agents.
351	
352	I recommend **option 2**, but with strict scope and deny-by-default behavior.
353	
354	A grant should include:
355	
356	```text
357	grant_id
358	principal_type       user | agent | task
359	principal_id
360	tool_id
361	capability
362	resource_scope
363	conversation_scope
364	workspace_scope
365	expires_at
366	created_by
367	approved_at
368	revoked_at
369	```
370	
371	Risk levels should be deterministic:
372	
373	```text
374	READ_ONLY
375	LOCAL_MUTATION
376	DESTRUCTIVE_LOCAL
377	EXTERNAL_WRITE
378	CREDENTIAL_ACCESS
379	DEPLOYMENT
380	FINANCIAL_OR_IRREVERSIBLE
381	```
382	
383	Rules I recommend:
384	
385	- Prompts cannot grant capabilities.
386	- An agent cannot approve its own action.
387	- A child task cannot widen its parent’s permissions.
388	- A reusable grant must be narrower than or equal to the requested action.
389	- Resource scope must be explicit, such as one repository, one path, or one provider.
390	- Approval decisions are immutable events; revocation affects future executions.
391	- Approval is rechecked when a queued task resumes, not only when it is created.
392	- A changed tool version or changed arguments invalidates a previous one-time approval.
393	- Secrets are never included in approval prompts or event payloads.
394	- The UI shows the exact tool, arguments summary, target resource, side effect, and expiry.
395	
396	Do you accept this reusable, scoped, deny-by-default model?

407	**Question 10: Container-local database**
408	
409	The user container needs durable records for chats, tasks, approvals, events, schedules, memories, provider metadata, and recovery checkpoints. Which database should it run?
410	
411	1. **SQLite with WAL mode, recommended:** embedded, fast, low operational cost, simple volume portability, and suitable for the prototype’s five-user limit.
412	2. **A PostgreSQL instance inside every user container:** stronger concurrent-write behavior, but heavier memory/storage use and more operational complexity.
413	3. **Redis plus files:** fast for runtime state, but weak as the durable source of truth and poor for querying/recovery.
414	4. **Central PostgreSQL for all product state:** easier centralized querying, but conflicts with the local-first boundary you chose.
415	
416	I recommend **SQLite with WAL mode**, behind repository interfaces.
417	
418	The local runtime should use:
419	
420	- One SQLite database per user container.
421	- WAL mode for concurrent reads and controlled writes.
422	- Short transactions.
423	- Explicit schema migrations.
424	- Append-only event journal tables.
425	- Foreign keys and uniqueness constraints enabled.
426	- Busy timeout and bounded retry for lock contention.
427	- No direct SQL from agent processes; all writes go through application repositories.
428	- Snapshot/restore as part of the user volume backup.
429	- A future repository implementation can move selected tables to central PostgreSQL without changing the domain model.
430	
431	The main risk is write contention. We should manage it with a single local persistence service or write coordinator for high-contention event/state writes, while allowing read-only queries from the web app and agents.
432	
433	Do you accept **SQLite with WAL mode** for the user-container database?

446	Should the user container expose one local runtime gateway that owns persistence and process coordination?

464	├── agent-process: general

710	**Question 14: Pi adapter transport**
711	
712	How should the TypeScript runtime gateway communicate with each Pi process?
713	
714	1. **Versioned JSON Lines over stdin/stdout, recommended for the prototype:** simple child-process supervision, easy local testing, natural streaming, and no extra listening port.
715	2. **Local authenticated HTTP/WebSocket server per Pi process:** clearer request/response semantics, but more ports, readiness checks, and lifecycle complexity.
716	3. **Shared Redis streams/pub-sub:** useful for distributed workers, but unnecessary inside one user container and harder to make process ownership explicit.
717	4. **Direct in-process library integration:** fastest calls, but weak crash isolation and poor portability.
718	
719	I recommend **option 1 for the Pi child-process adapter**, combined with local HTTP/WebSocket at the runtime gateway boundary.
720	
721	### Adapter protocol shape
722	
723	```text
724	Gateway -> Pi
725	  command
726	  command_id
727	  task_id
728	  conversation_id
729	  agent_id
730	  protocol_version
731	  payload
732	
733	Pi -> Gateway
734	  event
735	  event_id
736	  command_id
737	  task_id
738	  agent_id
739	  sequence
740	  protocol_version
741	  payload
742	```
743	
744	Required event types:
745	
746	```text
747	ready
748	heartbeat
749	message_delta
750	tool_proposed
751	progress
752	artifact_published
753	approval_requested
754	checkpoint
755	completed
756	failed
757	cancelled
758	fatal
759	```
760	
761	Required behavior:
762	
763	- stdout contains protocol messages only.
764	- stderr is diagnostic and redacted before logging.
765	- Every message has a correlation ID.
766	- The gateway detects process death independently of a final event.
767	- Heartbeats detect hung processes.
768	- The gateway can terminate the entire child process tree.
769	- Invalid messages are rejected and recorded as protocol failures.
770	- The protocol is versioned so Pi can evolve independently.
771	- Tests use fake Pi processes that emit deterministic events and failures.
772	
773	This gives the prototype a small, observable process contract without requiring a service mesh or additional ports.
774

859	**Question 16: Model routing and token budgets**
860	
861	How should Shera choose models and limit token usage?
862	
863	1. **User-selected model only:** the user chooses a provider/model for each agent or conversation; Shera does not automatically change it.
864	2. **Budget-aware routing, recommended:** the user sets a preferred model and maximum budget; the runtime starts with the lowest-cost suitable model and escalates only when deterministic rules or an agent request justify it.
865	3. **Automatic quality routing:** Shera chooses the model entirely based on task complexity, cost, and observed performance.
866	4. **One economical default:** use one low-cost model for the prototype, with manual provider switching for experiments.
867	
868	I recommend **option 2**.
869	
870	### Proposed routing policy
871	
872	Each task receives:
873	
874	```text
875	model_policy
876	preferred_provider
877	preferred_model
878	fallback_models
879	max_input_tokens
880	max_output_tokens
881	max_total_tokens
882	max_llm_calls
883	max_cost_estimate
884	escalation_policy
885	```
886	
887	The runtime can escalate when:
888	
889	- structured output fails repeatedly
890	- the agent reaches a configured iteration threshold
891	- the task requires a capability unavailable to the current model
892	- the agent explicitly requests escalation with a reason
893	- the user has allowed escalation
894	- a quality gate fails
895	
896	The runtime should not escalate merely because an agent “feels uncertain.”
897	
898	### Token-saving rules
899	
900	- Compile compact, role-specific context.
901	- Replace large outputs with artifact references.
902	- Avoid resending unchanged task plans.
903	- Truncate low-priority history deterministically.
904	- Keep tool schemas minimal and task-specific.
905	- Enforce per-task and per-agent budgets in the runtime gateway.
906	- Record input tokens, output tokens, model, provider, latency, and estimated cost.
907	- Pause or fail clearly when the budget is exhausted.
908	- Never silently switch to a model with materially different privacy or provider behavior.
909	
910	This keeps cost control in the deterministic runtime rather than relying on prompt instructions.
911	
912	Do you accept **budget-aware routing with a low-cost default and explicit escalation**?

938	- Default provider/model assignment from the deployment configuration
939	
940	The user then has two paths:
941	
942	1. **Use Shera immediately**
943	   - Safe defaults remain active.
944	   - The user can start chatting immediately.
945	   - No setup wizard blocks the first task.
946	
947	2. **Run onboarding**
948	   - An ordered wizard explains and optionally changes settings.
949	   - It covers provider/model, agent behavior, permissions, notifications, memory, workspace, and autonomy.
950	   - Each step can keep the recommended default.
951	   - The user can skip onboarding and reopen it later.
952	
953	### Full configuration editor
954	
955	The settings interface should expose all configuration categories:
956	
957	```text
958	Account
959	Agents
960	Providers and models
961	Model budgets
962	Tools
963	Permissions and approvals
964	Workspace

966	Memory

970	Runtime and resource tier

974	
975	Internally, onboarding and settings must use the same configuration commands and schemas. Onboarding should not have a separate configuration implementation.
976	
977	The UI should show:
978	
979	```text
980	Recommended default
981	Current value
982	Why it matters
983	Risk or cost impact
984	Reset to default
985	```
986	
987	Advanced settings can be progressively disclosed so a new user sees a simple interface while an experienced user can configure everything.
988	
989	### Important constraint
990	
991	“Automatic setup” must not mean silently inventing credentials or silently selecting a provider with unexpected privacy or cost behavior. Defaults must come from an explicit deployment policy, local model availability, or user-provided configuration.
992	
993	**Question 17: What happens when no model provider is configured?**
994	
995	Choose the default behavior:
996	
997	1. **Admin-configured provider required:** the deployment supplies a default provider/model; users see a ready-to-use system.
998	2. **Local-first fallback, recommended:** use a locally available model such as Ollama when configured; otherwise use the deployment default.
999	3. **Mock mode fallback:** allow the UI and workflow to run with deterministic fake responses until the user configures a real provider.
1000	4. **Ask during first message:** do not block onboarding, but pause the first real task and request provider setup.
1001	
1002	My recommendation is **option 2 plus option 3 for development**:
1003	
1004	- Production-like deployments use an admin-configured provider.
1005	- Local deployments use Ollama when available.
1006	- Development and test environments can use a deterministic mock provider.
1007	- If no real provider exists, the UI clearly says that the runtime is ready but model access is not configured.

1029	How should Shera support future agent sets for different audiences while keeping the runtime stable?

1032	2. **Versioned agent packs, recommended:** an agent pack is a declarative, versioned bundle containing profiles, prompts, tools, capabilities, default settings, onboarding questions, and UI metadata.
1033	3. **Fully user-created agents:** users define all agent behavior, tools, prompts, and permissions themselves.
1034	4. **Plugin marketplace first:** agent sets are installed as third-party plugins.

1039

1041	AgentPack
1042	├── manifest
1043	├── agent profiles
1044	├── prompt fragments
1045	├── capability declarations
1046	├── tool allow-list

1078

1103	**Question 19: Agent-pack assignment scope**
1104	
1105	How should a pack be assigned?
1106	
1107	1. **One pack per deployment:** a deployment serves one customer type at a time.
1108	2. **Multiple packs per deployment, one pack per user, recommended:** the installation can serve students, developers, researchers, and other audiences; each user is assigned one pinned pack.
1109	3. **Multiple packs per deployment, one pack per workspace:** a user can have different workspaces with different agent packs.
1110	4. **Pack can change per conversation:** each conversation selects its own pack.
1111

1114	### Why workspace-level assignment is the strongest boundary
1115	
1116	A user may be:
1117	
1118	- a student in one workspace
1119	- a developer in another
1120	- a researcher in a third
1121	
1122	Each workspace would have:
1123

1125	workspace_id
1126	agent_pack_id
1127	agent_pack_version
1128	configuration_snapshot
1129	provider_policy
1130	tool_policy
1131	memory_policy

1133	
1134	Rules:
1135	
1136	- A task is permanently associated with the workspace’s pack version.
1137	- Pack changes affect new tasks only.
1138	- Existing conversations and tasks keep their original pack version.
1139	- Agents from different packs do not silently collaborate in the same task.
1140	- Cross-pack collaboration requires an explicit bridge policy from the control plane.
1141	- User permissions can restrict which packs are available.
1142	- The runtime loads packs from a registry rather than branching on customer names.
1143	- Pack manifests cannot override global security, tenancy, approval, or resource rules.
1144	
1145	This lets Shera support future customer segments without maintaining separate forks of the platform.

1168	**Question 20: Credits and provider billing**
1169	
1170	How should model usage be accounted for?
1171	
1172	1. **Simple balance counter, recommended for the prototype:** admin assigns credits; every platform-provider request deducts an estimated or recorded cost; tasks pause when the balance is exhausted.
1173	2. **Reservation and settlement ledger:** reserve estimated cost before each request, settle against actual usage afterward, and release unused reservations.
1174	3. **Usage-only tracking:** record token and cost usage but allow tasks to continue even when the balance reaches zero.
1175	4. **Separate budgets per agent/workspace:** every agent or workspace receives its own independent allowance.
1176	
1177	I recommend **option 2 internally, with a simple UI**.
1178	
1179	The user should see only:
1180	
1181	```text
1182	Available credits
1183	Used credits
1184	Estimated task cost
1185	Provider/model
1186	Cost source: platform credit or custom provider
1187	```
1188	
1189	Internally, use an append-only ledger:
1190	
1191	```text
1192	credit_account
1193	credit_transaction
1194	usage_record
1195	cost_estimate
1196	reservation
1197	settlement
1198	release
1199	```
1200	
1201	Rules:
1202	
1203	- Admin grants credits through the control plane.
1204	- Platform-provider requests reserve a bounded estimate before execution.
1205	- Actual provider usage settles the reservation.
1206	- Unused reservation is released.
1207	- If reservation cannot be made, the task pauses before calling the model.
1208	- Custom-provider requests do not consume Shera credits, but usage and latency are still recorded locally.
1209	- Provider failures must release reservations unless the provider may have processed the request.
1210	- Ambiguous provider outcomes must not be blindly retried.
1211	- Credits cannot authorize tools, filesystem writes, deployment, or external actions. Those remain separate approval capabilities.
1212	- Existing tasks retain their provider/model snapshot unless the user explicitly changes them.
1213	
1214	For the first version, should the user have:
1215	
1216	- one account-level credit balance, or
1217	- separate balances per workspace?

1267	**Question 22: Provider credential custody**
1268	
1269	How should provider credentials be stored and delivered?
1270	
1271	1. **Deployment environment only for admin providers; encrypted local container storage for user providers, recommended:** admin keys stay in deployment/runtime secret configuration, while user custom-provider keys are encrypted inside the user container and injected only for the provider call.
1272	2. **All provider keys stored centrally:** the control plane owns and injects every key.
1273	3. **All provider keys stored in each container:** even admin-provided credentials are copied into every user container.
1274	4. **Agent-managed credentials:** agents can create, read, or update provider credentials through tools.
1275	
1276	I recommend **option 1**.
1277	
1278	### Platform/admin provider
1279	
1280	- Configured by the administrator or deployment operator.
1281	- Stored in the deployment secret manager or protected environment.
1282	- Never written to SQLite, central PostgreSQL, prompts, memories, artifacts, or logs.
1283	- Runtime gateway receives only the provider configuration it needs.
1284	- Usage is charged against the account credit ledger.
1285	- Provider/model catalog is exposed as metadata, not secrets.
1286	
1287	### User custom provider
1288	
1289	- Stored encrypted inside the user container.
1290	- Encryption key comes from the container’s protected secret/bootstrap mechanism.
1291	- The runtime gateway decrypts only at call time.
1292	- Agents receive a provider reference, never the raw credential.
1293	- The UI displays masked values and supports revoke/replace.
1294	- Custom-provider configuration is workspace-scoped or account-scoped by explicit user choice.
1295	- Custom provider requests do not consume Shera credits.
1296	
1297	### Non-negotiable rules
1298	
1299	- Agent packs cannot contain secrets.
1300	- Prompts and event payloads contain provider IDs, not credentials.
1301	- Error handling redacts headers, authorization fields, URLs with embedded secrets, and provider response bodies where needed.
1302	- Provider keys are excluded from backups unless the backup is explicitly encrypted and authorized.
1303	- A provider configuration change affects new tasks; existing tasks keep a provider snapshot or fail safely if the credential was revoked.
1304	
1305	Do you accept **deployment secrets for admin providers plus encrypted container-local storage for user custom providers**?

1438	===== [74] ASSISTANT @ 2026-09-11T03:46:58.776Z =====
1439	## Repository comparison result
1440	
1441	I would **not** use the earlier 20-package structure exactly as proposed. It is directionally sound, but too fragmented for a first implementation. Too many packages create:
1442	
1443	- more dependency edges
1444	- more build configuration
1445	- more places for agents to search
1446	- more versioning friction
1447	- more “where does this belong?” decisions
1448	
1449	The best strategy is:
1450	
1451	> **Three deployable applications, a small set of shared contract packages, and feature modules inside each application.**
1452	
1453	This preserves future scale without turning the prototype into a mini-platform before the vertical slice exists.
1454	
1455	### What each repository teaches us
1456	
1457	| Repository | Strongest workspace practice | What Shera should take | What to avoid |
1458	|---|---|---|---|
1459	| OpenHands / Agent Canvas | Explicit repository boundaries, typed client/server separation, mock/live test separation, backend registry | Backend registry, typed clients, fake runtime, live-test isolation, directory ownership rules | Starting with multiple repositories and too many independently released packages |
1460	| Agent Zero | Declarative profiles, project-scoped configuration, deterministic extension ordering, local instructions | Versioned agent packs, profile-local prompts/tools, deterministic discovery, scoped configuration | Unbounded dynamic extensions and prompt-only security |
1461	| LibreChat | Monorepo packages, generated client calls, reusable authorization middleware, queued turns, persisted/sanitized errors | Typed API clients, resource authorization, queue commands, durable user-visible errors | Provider/integration breadth before the core runtime is stable |
1462	| Dify | API/worker/runtime separation, typed event adaptation, stop-aware queue behavior, generated deployment configuration | Durable event contracts, cancellation at queue boundaries, worker separation, migration checks | Full workflow graph machinery and a large optional-service matrix |
1463	| SuperAGI | Explicit execution records, persisted statuses, schedules, permissions, migrations, operator history | Task state model, execution records, schedules, run history | Unsafe queue serialization, broad exception-to-string handling, legacy monolith patterns |
1464	
1465	OpenHands keeps frontend, SDK/server, typed client, and automation responsibilities separate, and its test system intentionally distinguishes mock-LLM E2E from live LLM tests. That is the best model for Shera’s backend boundary and test discipline. ([github.com](https://github.com/OpenHands/OpenHands/blob/main/AGENTS.md))
1466	
1467	Agent Zero’s current structure is particularly useful for future customer-specific agent packs: profile metadata, prompts, tools, projects, and settings are treated as separate scopes, and its extension guidance emphasizes deterministic ordering and narrow overrides. ([github.com](https://github.com/agent0ai/agent-zero))
1468	
1469	LibreChat shows how a growing product can centralize API packages, generated clients, access-control middleware, queued turns, and sanitized persisted errors. Those are useful patterns for Shera’s control plane and web application, but copying its entire integration surface would make the prototype harder to maintain. ([github.com](https://github.com/langgenius/dify/blob/main/web/AGENTS.md))
1470	
1471	Dify’s strongest transferable ideas are execution boundaries: worker/API separation, typed event adaptation, stop-aware queue scheduling, and deployment configuration generated from controlled sources. Its runtime also demonstrates path isolation for agent jobs. ([github.com](https://github.com/langgenius/dify/blob/main/dify-agent/docs/dify-agent/guide/index.md))
1472	
1473	SuperAGI is valuable mainly as a data-model reference: explicit execution statuses, schedules, permissions, current workflow steps, and operator-visible run history. Its older implementation quality is uneven, so use its concepts, not its queue or error-handling code.
1474

### 48. TOOL RESULT

1	# Shera Bot Prototype Product Requirements Document
2	
3	**Status:** Approved prototype specification  
4	**Date:** 2026-09-08  
5	**Scope:** Private prototype for validating a multi-agent, Grok Bot-inspired assistant
6	
7	## 1. Product definition
8	
9	Shera Bot is an independent, general-purpose AI assistant platform inspired by existing products such as Grok Bot. It provides three specialized AI agents, persistent user workspaces, tool use, autonomous tasks, multi-agent collaboration, provider/model choice, and transparent execution through a Telegram-like web interface.
10	
11	The product uses its own name, branding, UI, prompts, system behavior, and implementation. It must not copy Grok or xAI trademarks, proprietary code, private infrastructure, protected assets, or internal architecture. Open-source components are used only under their applicable licenses.
12	
13	The prototype validates the end-to-end experience and runtime architecture before expanding the platform. It is private testing software, not a production-scale launch.
14	
15	## 2. Goals and non-goals
16	
17	### 2.1 Goals
18	
19	1. Validate a Telegram-like dashboard for persistent agent chats.
20	2. Validate three independent Pi-based agents with distinct roles and private identities.
21	3. Validate expandable workspaces with files, terminal, browser, task activity, and tool results.
22	4. Validate user-triggered and autonomous agent work.
23	5. Validate multi-agent delegation and transparent group chats.
24	6. Validate user-selected AI providers, models, and custom OpenAI-compatible endpoints.
25	7. Validate local-first user data storage inside isolated user containers.
26	8. Validate dynamic approval for risky actions.
27	9. Validate restart recovery, backups, and container portability.
28	10. Produce evidence for deciding whether to build the larger platform.
29	
30	### 2.2 Explicit prototype non-goals
31	
32	- More than five users.
33	- More than one cloud VM.
34	- Native mobile apps or PWA packaging.
35	- Real billing or subscriptions.
36	- X/social search.
37	- Full desktop takeover or user browser/terminal control.
38	- Model training, fine-tuning, or weight modification.
39	- Multi-region deployment or production high availability.
40	- Enterprise organizations, team administration, or full audit UI.
41	- Guaranteed correctness of agent outputs.
42	- Unrestricted autonomous external actions.
43	- User data export or permanent data purge.
44	- Public tool marketplace.
45	- Provider guarantees beyond configured APIs.
46	
47	## 3. Users, access, and tiers
48	
49	### 3.1 Audience priority
50	
51	1. Researchers.
52	2. Individual developers.
53	3. Students.
54	
55	The product remains general-purpose and supports any research discipline.
56	
57	### 3.2 Authentication and access
58	
59	- Google OAuth is the only login method.
60	- Open signup is allowed until five accounts are registered.
61	- Signup is refused after the five-account cap.
62	- Administrator status is a manually managed database flag.
63	- No real payments are processed.
64	- Light, Balanced, and Heavy tiers are simulated and may be assigned by a user or administrator.
65	- Balanced is the development and prototype default.
66	
67	### 3.3 Resource tiers
68	
69	| Tier | CPU | RAM | Storage |
70	|---|---:|---:|---:|
71	| Light | 1 vCPU | 2 GB | 10 GB |
72	| Balanced | 2 vCPU | 4 GB | 25 GB |
73	| Heavy | 4 vCPU | 8 GB | 50 GB |
74	
75	## 4. User experience
76	
77	### 4.1 Dashboard
78	
79	After login, the user lands on a simple Telegram-like dashboard with a sidebar. The sidebar contains persistent Researcher, Developer, and General Assistant chats, user-created task chats, and automatically created group chats. It shows unread, running, approval-required, failed, and completed states.
80	
81	The user can message an agent directly, create a task-specific chat, create a group chat, or follow an agent-created group chat after delegation.
82	
83	### 4.2 Chat types
84	
85	**Agent chat:** persistent conversation with one fixed-role agent. It has access to that agent's private profile folder, the shared workspace, and relevant shared task/system information.
86	
87	**Task-specific chat:** user-defined task conversation that may involve one or more agents. At completion the user may keep, archive, or delete it. Kept chats auto-archive after seven days. Deleted chats are soft-deleted and retained indefinitely.
88	
89	**Multi-agent group chat:** created by the user or an agent. It includes the user, orchestrator, and participating agents. The orchestrator may add or remove agents as the task evolves. The user may message the group or address any individual agent directly. Agent messages, progress, tool activity, approvals, and results are visible.
90	
91	After a group task completes, the user is notified and the group chat is archived as read-only. It cannot be continued directly.
92	
93	### 4.3 Workspace
94	
95	Chat is the default view. Each chat has an expandable workspace panel, similar to an agent harness, and the workspace can open as a separate full-page view.
96	
97	The prototype workspace includes a file/folder browser, file editor, terminal panel, interactive browser panel, task/progress panel, tool-call history/results, shared files, and private agent files.
98	
99	The user receives a live shared read-only view of browser and terminal activity. Direct takeover is future work. The UI must prevent conflicting simultaneous control.
100	
101	## 5. Agents
102	
103	All agents run as separate Pi processes in one user container. Each has a fixed role with editable user instructions, a private profile folder, private memory, role defaults, and access to the shared workspace and shared task context.
104	
105	### 5.1 Researcher
106	
107	The Researcher investigates questions and produces evidence-backed outputs. It searches and reads public web sources, compares authority and recency, extracts claims and quotations, reads uploaded PDFs/documents/spreadsheets/images, creates literature reviews and reports, manages citations and source indexes, monitors configured sources, and delegates coding/data work to Developer or synthesis work to General Assistant.
108	
109	Default tools include web search/page fetch, interactive browser, document/PDF parsing, image understanding, spreadsheet/data analysis, file tools, notes, report generation, schedules, event monitoring, and delegation.
110	
111	It may read shared files, write research notes/reports/artifacts, and read/write its private folder. Destructive and external-write actions require approval. Outputs include citations, evidence tables, reports, confidence statements, and unresolved questions. Its identity is evidence-driven and explicit about uncertainty.
112	
113	### 5.2 Developer
114	
115	The Developer inspects repositories, creates and modifies code, runs tests/linters/type checks/builds, diagnoses regressions, creates scripts and integrations, runs technical experiments, operates browsers and terminals, manages branches/worktrees/commits/patches, generates documentation, monitors repositories/files/builds/webhooks, and creates reusable tools.
116	
117	Default tools include file browser/editor, terminal, sandboxed code execution, Git, browser automation, web search, document/PDF parsing, spreadsheet/data analysis, image understanding, artifact generation, schedules, event monitoring, and delegation.
118	
119	It may read/write assigned project workspaces and its private folder, run non-destructive commands automatically, and execute code inside the user container. Repository mutation, deletion, package installation, deployment, and external writes require approval.
120	
121	Coding work uses an agent-specific branch and separate worktree. Related conflicting work is queued until the earlier change finishes. The main orchestrator prepares merges and asks the user before final merge.
122	
123	### 5.3 General Assistant
124	
125	The General Assistant handles general questions, planning, coordination, user-facing synthesis, memory, task creation, and group orchestration. It clarifies goals, chooses whether one or multiple agents are needed, delegates to Researcher and Developer, combines results, writes and formats content, manages notes/checklists/documents, tracks approvals, and produces final answers/artifacts.
126	
127	Default tools include orchestration, shared files, web search/browser, document/PDF tools, image understanding, spreadsheet/data analysis, artifacts, schedules, monitoring, delegation, and tool inventory.
128	
129	It may read shared files, read/write its private folder, create plans/notes/artifacts, coordinate tasks, and read agent results. It may not silently modify another agent's private files. Destructive and external-write actions require approval. It is transparent about delegated work.
130	
131	## 6. Runtime and tenancy
132	
133	Each user receives one isolated container acting as that user's cloud computer. It contains three Pi processes, one shared workspace, three private profile folders, shared task/group/system information, user-local provider and repository credentials, one shared browser profile, and persistent task/memory/artifact/tool state.
134	
135	All three agents may work independently or collaborate on one task. Full parallelism is allowed subject to CPU, memory, process, and storage limits. When resources are exhausted, running work continues and new jobs queue.
136	
137	### 6.1 Deployment
138	
139	Local development uses Docker Compose. The real prototype uses one Ubuntu 24.04 cloud VM running Docker Compose with the web app, Express control-plane API, PostgreSQL, Redis, reverse proxy, backup service, and up to five user containers.
140	
141	The runtime API is provider-neutral and portable so a user container can later move to its own VM. Product behavior must not depend on Docker-specific paths or APIs.
142	
143	## 7. Tools and agent-created tools
144	
145	Prototype-functional tools are web search/page reading, interactive browser, file upload/read/write/edit/share, terminal, sandboxed code execution, PDF/document parsing, spreadsheet/data analysis, image understanding, Git clone/import/branches/worktrees/validation/merge preparation, artifact generation, delegation/group orchestration, schedules, file-change monitoring, memory, tool inventory, and dynamic approvals.
146	
147	Minimal external API tools have a name, base URL, HTTP method, path, headers/body template, and credential reference. Read requests run automatically. Writes/deletes require approval. Calls have timeouts and response-size limits, appear in redacted history, and can be disabled.
148	
149	Agents may create and activate non-destructive tools automatically. Risky tools require approval. The creating agent selects private or shared inventory scope. Tools run in the same user container in the prototype. Each tool exposes its name, purpose, owner, scope, permissions, and version.
150	
151	Future integrations include broad webhook/event builders, advanced external connectors, specialized parsers/artifact formats, advanced tool signing/sandboxing/distribution, native clients, full desktop takeover, and additional Pi integrations.
152	
153	## 8. Autonomy, tasks, and approvals
154	
155	Agents support user-triggered and autonomous work. Autonomous triggers include schedules, shared-file changes, private-folder changes, Git changes, incoming webhooks, completion of another agent's task, agent-evaluated conditions, and external service events.
156	
157	Read-only and non-destructive actions may run automatically. Risky actions include destructive file operations, repository mutation, external writes/deletes, messages, deployments, purchases, and similar side effects. Risky actions pause for approval by default.
158	
159	Approval choices are: **Approve this execution only**, **Always approve this tool**, **Always approve this tool for this agent**, **Reject this action**, and **Cancel the task**. Permissions are reviewable and revocable in settings.
160	
161	Approval requests wait indefinitely. Reminders occur immediately, at 15 minutes, at 45 minutes, at 3 hours, and at 24 hours. No reminders occur after 24 hours. Turning reminders off stops notifications only; the task stays paused until approval or rejection.
162	
163	Tasks retry transient failures with bounded backoff up to three times, then pause and notify. Immediate cancellation stops parent, child, and queued work while preserving logs and partial files.
164	
165	## 9. Providers and models
166	
167	Supported providers are native OpenAI, Anthropic, and Google adapters; Ollama/local models; generic OpenAI-compatible endpoints; and user-defined custom providers.
168	
169	Custom provider fields are display name, protocol type, base URL, API key or no-key mode, model identifier, optional organization/project, optional headers, optional request parameters, connection test, enable/disable, and per-agent/per-conversation assignment.
170	
171	Each agent has a default provider/model. Users may override the provider/model per conversation. Provider keys are local-first inside the user container, runtime-injected, masked, revocable, and never written to prompts, chat history, memory, tool history, logs, shared files, or tool inventory. The central service does not persist provider keys.
172	
173	## 10. Projects and Git
174	
175	Users may upload/import project archives or clone repositories from Git URLs. Private repository credentials are provided per repository, stored locally inside the user container, and scoped to that repository.
176	
177	There is no artificial project-count limit. Capacity is limited by the user container. Projects are shared by default, with optional restriction to selected agents.
178	
179	Coding agents use separate branches and worktrees. Non-coding agents publish notes, research, reports, and artifacts through the shared task area. Related conflicting work is queued rather than concurrently merged.
180	
181	The orchestrator prepares merge requests and runs available validation. The minimal merge approval view shows only the change summary, changed files, validation status, conflict status, and target branch. The user may approve, reject, or request changes. The orchestrator never silently merges into the main branch.
182	
183	## 11. Memory and learning
184	
185	Runtime learning only is supported. Models are not fine-tuned and weights are not modified.
186	
187	There is one shared user/workspace memory and one private memory per agent. Agents automatically update memory from conversations, tasks, files, outcomes, and corrections. Users may view, edit, update, delete, permanently forget, disable automatic memory globally or per agent, inspect sources, and restore earlier versions.
188	
189	Passwords, API keys, tokens, payment data, and similar secrets must never be saved to memory.
190	
191	## 12. Data, privacy, storage, and backups
192	
193	Conversations, files, projects/worktrees, memories, browser state, tasks, group chats, tool inventory, provider keys, repository credentials, and artifacts remain inside the user container. The central service stores only account identity, Google OAuth identity, container mapping, and minimal operational metadata.
194	
195	Warn when storage exceeds 80%. Never automatically delete user files. At 100%, block actions that create or expand data while preserving chat, reading, deletion, export, and cleanup.
196	
197	Snapshot each user persistent volume every six hours and retain the latest seven snapshots. Administrators may trigger restore. User data export is future work.
198	
199	## 13. Restart and recovery
200	
201	Persistent volumes remount automatically. All Pi processes restart. Queued tasks resume from durable state. Interrupted tasks resume from a safe checkpoint or pause for review. Group-chat history and membership restore. Schedules and event watchers restart without duplication. Pending approvals remain pending. Browser profile/cookies restore. Potentially duplicated external effects are not blindly replayed. The dashboard shows recovery status. If safe recovery cannot be proven, the task pauses and notifies.
202	
203	## 14. Real-time transport and notifications
204	
205	WebSockets handle chat streaming, agent/group messages, progress, tool activity, approvals, notifications, browser/terminal events, and recovery status. HTTP handles Google OAuth, settings, providers, files, projects, task commands, history, and administration.
206	
207	Notifications are live updates in the open workspace, in-app completion/failure/approval notifications, and browser notifications when the tab is open but unfocused. Users can enable/disable browser and in-app notifications, mute one approval, one task, or all approval reminders. Pending approvals stay visible when reminders are muted.
208	
209	Tasks continue after browser closure. Reconnect reloads durable history and replays missed events from the last acknowledged cursor. Multiple tabs synchronize. Commands are idempotent. Approval decisions propagate across tabs. The UI identifies live, replayed, delayed, and disconnected events.
210	
211	## 15. Admin console
212	
213	The manually flagged administrator can list users, enable/disable accounts, assign tiers, provision/restart/stop containers, view container status and resource usage, trigger backups and restore one of seven snapshots, emergency-cancel all tasks for one user, and view recent container/task failures.
214	
215	Security-relevant actions are logged internally even without a full audit UI.
216	
217	## 16. Network and security
218	
219	User containers may access the public internet for research and approved services, but have no inbound public access. The control plane communicates with containers through an authenticated internal runtime channel. Containers cannot access other users' containers, volumes, processes, or credentials. Agents cannot access host Docker sockets or host filesystem paths. Localhost services are scoped to the user container.
220	
221	Network actions are classified as read-only or external-write. External writes and risky actions require approval. Provider keys, Git credentials, and browser cookies never appear in prompts, logs, memory, messages, or tool inventory. All logs redact sensitive values.
222	
223	## 17. Technology stack
224	
225	- Next.js, React, TypeScript, and Tailwind CSS for the web application.
226	- Express.js with TypeScript for the control-plane API.
227	- PostgreSQL for central metadata, durable task routing, and operational state.
228	- Redis for transient queues, pub/sub, and live-event support.
229	- WebSockets plus HTTP.
230	- Docker Compose locally and on the single cloud VM.
231	- pnpm/Turborepo TypeScript monorepo.
232	- Zod shared schemas.
233	- TypeScript adapter around the product-maintained Pi fork.
234	
235	Low-level library versions, exact cloud provider, database table names, module boundaries, and visual details remain engineering/design decisions.
236	
237	## 18. Quality baseline
238	
239	The prototype must work on current Chrome, Edge, Firefox, and Safari; support responsive layouts from approximately 360px mobile width through large desktop; provide keyboard-accessible core flows, visible focus states, meaningful labels, loading/empty/error/disconnected states, automatic reconnect, missed-event replay, bounded payloads, redacted errors, idempotent commands, pagination/virtualization for long lists, health checks, and clear degradation when providers/tools are unavailable.
240	
241	Normal command acknowledgement target is approximately 500ms under normal local/cloud conditions. No formal WCAG certification, multi-region SLA, production high availability, or full load-testing program is required for prototype acceptance.
242	
243	## 19. Prototype acceptance criteria
244	
245	1. Google OAuth login works and the five-account cap is enforced.
246	2. A Balanced-tier user container is provisioned.
247	3. Three agent chats are available.
248	4. Each agent runs as a separate Pi process with its own private profile folder.
249	5. User-created task chats work.
250	6. Agents can create group chats and delegate work.
251	7. Live agent messages, progress, tool calls, approvals, and results are visible.
252	8. Prototype-functional tools work end-to-end.
253	9. Agents can create reusable private/shared tools under the approval policy.
254	10. Built-in, local, OpenAI-compatible, and custom providers can be configured and tested.
255	11. Per-agent defaults and per-conversation provider overrides work.
256	12. At least one autonomous scheduled or event-triggered task completes.
257	13. Risky actions pause and display the defined approval choices and reminder sequence.
258	14. Transient failures retry no more than three times before pausing and notifying.
259	15. Immediate cancellation stops parent, child, and queued work while preserving partial outputs.
260	16. Shared/private files, chats, tasks, memories, browser state, and tools persist across restart.
261	17. A coding task uses a separate branch/worktree and asks approval before merge.
262	18. Automatic backups run and an administrator restores one snapshot.
263	19. Restart recovery restores processes, state, schedules, browser profile, and approvals safely.
264	20. Desktop and mobile web layouts work on target browsers.
265	21. No critical cross-user isolation or credential-leak failure is observed.
266	
267	## 20. Future work register
268	
269	- Native iOS/Android app and installable PWA enhancements.
270	- Full browser/desktop takeover.
271	- X/social search, social feeds, voice, image generation, video generation, and richer multimodal workflows.
272	- Production billing, subscriptions, usage metering, and cost controls.
273	- User/admin data export, permanent purge, and configurable retention.
274	- Enterprise organizations, teams, roles, and audit interface.
275	- Multi-region deployment, high availability, autoscaling, and per-user VM orchestration.
276	- Managed production secrets service.
277	- Advanced connector/OAuth framework and webhook/event-condition builder.
278	- Tool review, signing, sandboxing, marketplace, and distribution.
279	- Model routing, fallback, quotas, and scale cost optimization.
280	- Distributed tracing, external error monitoring, formal load testing, and performance budgets.
281	- Cross-device synchronization for local-first data.
282	- Cloud-provider migration automation.
283

332	## 23. Open-source and legal compliance
333	
334	The project follows strict compliance. It preserves every dependency's license, copyright notices, attribution, and source/disclosure obligations; maintains a dependency/license inventory; reviews Pi fork obligations and modifications; and does not present open-source work as proprietary. Product branding and behavior remain independent from Grok/xAI.
335	
336	## 24. Decision boundary
337	
338	This document is implementation-ready for the private prototype. Engineering may choose low-level libraries, exact versions, cloud provider, table names, module boundaries, and visual details without reopening product decisions. Changes to user-visible scope, data ownership, approval safety, tenancy, agent roles, persistence, or acceptance criteria require an explicit product decision and an update to this document.

### 49. TOOL RESULT

1	## Shera Bot landing page expansion plan
2	
3	### Scope boundary
4	- Modify files only inside `E:\PROJECTS\shera-bot\business-landing-portfolio`.
5	- Leave the root PRD, repository skill files, tooling state, and other sibling paths unchanged.
6	- Keep the existing lightweight Next.js + Tailwind + Lucide stack; do not add Framer Motion, GSAP, Lottie, or another animation dependency unless verification proves a native implementation insufficient.
7	
8	### 1. Rebalance the page around non-technical professionals
9	The current page leads with product mechanics. Reorder and expand the page so approximately 70% of the visible content communicates outcomes, everyday use, and future value, while approximately 30% explains how Shera Bot works.
10	
11	Benefit-led sections to add or rewrite:
12	
13	1. **Hero promise**
14	   - Keep the approved headline direction, but make the supporting copy universal: work, planning, research, writing, follow-up, and coordination — not coding-first language.
15	   - Add a compact supporting line such as “From the first question to the finished outcome, without stitching together five different tools.”
16	
17	2. **Audience/use-case signal strip**
18	   - Add a moving-but-optional category rail for everyday work: Plan, Research, Write, Analyze, Organize, Follow up.
19	   - Keep it understandable to a non-CSE visitor and avoid technical jargon.
20	
21	3. **Everyday work use-case gallery**
22	   - Add six benefit cards with concrete scenarios and outcomes:
23	     - Meeting-to-action: turn notes into owners, follow-ups, and a clear next step.
24	     - Research-to-brief: compare sources and return a decision-ready summary.
25	     - Idea-to-plan: turn a rough idea into a milestone-based plan.
26	     - Content-to-publish: draft, adapt, and organize content for different channels.
27	     - Data-to-decision: explain a spreadsheet/report and surface what deserves attention.
28	     - Inbox-to-priority: group requests, identify urgency, and prepare responses.
29	   - Phrase these as illustrative use cases rather than unsupported claims that the product already performs every integration.
30	
31	4. **“Stop tool-hopping” comparison section**
32	   - Explain that Shera Bot is intended to reduce context switching between chatbots, notes, search tabs, documents, task lists, and follow-up tools.
33	   - Use a visual before/after composition: scattered tasks on one side, one connected workspace on the other.
34	   - Avoid naming competitors or making a literal replacement guarantee; position it as a unified work layer.
35	
36	5. **A day with Shera Bot timeline**
37	   - Show morning planning, mid-day research/writing, afternoon coordination, and end-of-day review.
38	   - Connect each moment to a concrete benefit: less restarting, fewer dropped details, clearer next actions, and continuity tomorrow.
39	
40	6. **Role-based benefit row**
41	   - Add cards for non-technical professionals such as founder/manager, researcher/analyst, marketer/creator, operations/people, and student/independent professional.
42	   - Each card should answer “what gets easier?” rather than describing implementation internals.
43	
44	7. **Compounding workspace / future value section**
45	   - Strengthen the existing private workspace story around continuity: the value grows because work, preferences, decisions, and project context stay connected.
46	   - Make early-access benefits explicit without inventing pricing, customer counts, testimonials, or launch dates: early access news, roadmap influence, and first access to the complete workflow.
47	
48	### 2. Keep the remaining 30% as product proof
49	Retain and refine the mechanics sections, but make them shorter and subordinate to the benefit story:
50	
51	- **Hero-adjacent illustrative chat preview:** keep the Telegram-like demo as a visual teaser, but label it clearly as illustrative.
52	- **How Shera works:** keep a concise three-step flow: ask → agents work → review.
53	- **Meet the agents:** keep Researcher, Developer, and General Assistant, but rewrite descriptions in plain-language outcomes.
54	- **Private workspace safeguards:** keep the isolated workspace, memory, files, and approval explanation, using careful “designed to” language.
55	- **FAQ:** expand to answer non-technical objections: “Do I need to know how to code?”, “Can I use it for ordinary work?”, “Will it replace my current tools?”, “What happens to my email?”, “When can I try it?”, and “Which models will it support?”
56	- **Final beta CTA:** use a benefit-led invitation rather than another architecture explanation.
57	
58	### 3. Add purposeful animation without a heavy dependency
59	
60	1. **Fix the existing demo reveal**
61	   - Replace the unsupported `animate-in`, `fade-in`, and `slide-in-from-bottom-2` classes with a local `.demo-message-enter` keyframe using opacity and translateY only.
62	   - Gate the timed transcript with `prefers-reduced-motion` and pause it when the document is hidden. Reduced-motion users should see the complete stable transcript immediately.
63	   - Keep the preview out of `aria-live`; provide a stable accessible description for assistive technology.
64	
65	2. **Add scroll entrance reveals**
66	   - Create a small client `Reveal` component using `IntersectionObserver`.
67	   - Render content normally so it remains available without JavaScript; use `.reveal-ready` / `.reveal-visible` only as progressive enhancement.
68	   - Reveal section headings/cards with opacity plus 12–20px translateY, 400–600ms duration, and small staggered delays. Do not animate layout dimensions or height.
69	
70	3. **Add restrained visual motion**
71	   - Animate one or two decorative halo/orbit layers with slow transform/opacity movement.
72	   - Add subtle floating motion to selected use-case/workspace badges.
73	   - Add a lightweight animated gradient or shimmer treatment to the primary beta CTA.
74	   - Keep all motion `motion-safe`, avoid animating blur/backdrop-filter/box-shadow, and do not imply the illustrative preview is live.
75	
76	4. **Respect accessibility and performance**
77	   - Preserve the global reduced-motion rule and add a JavaScript matchMedia fallback for the transcript and reveal component.
78	   - Pause offscreen/nonessential loops where practical.
79	   - Add `aria-hidden` to decorative orbital layers and a concise text equivalent for the demo.
80	   - Keep animation timings interruptible and limited to opacity/transform.
81	
82	### 4. Improve navigation and conversion structure
83	- Add IDs for every major section and update the navbar anchors to cover `Product`, `Use cases`, `Agents`, and `FAQ` (or an equivalent compact set).
84	- Add a compact mobile navigation treatment rather than hiding all section links below `md`.
85	- Keep the waitlist CTA visible at all widths.
86	- Add a skip-to-content link and ensure focus styles remain visible.
87	- Add a small “what happens next” line beneath the final form so visitors know the email is only for early-access communication.
88	
89	### 5. Content and visual implementation shape
90	- Keep the data-driven arrays for agents and FAQs, and add data arrays for use cases, role-based outcomes, day-in-the-life steps, and tool-hopping comparisons.
91	- Extract repeated benefit/section wrappers into small components where that makes the page easier to maintain; do not over-abstract one-off visual compositions.
92	- Preserve the approved cobalt design tokens and Space Grotesk / DM Sans / JetBrains Mono system.
93	- Use Lucide icons consistently; no emoji or unlicensed visual assets.
94	- Keep the screenshot-inspired dark glass aesthetic, but make content hierarchy and benefit readability stronger than decoration.
95	
96	### 6. Verification
97	- Run `npm run lint`, `npm run typecheck`, and `npm run build` from `business-landing-portfolio`.
98	- Run a browser smoke pass at desktop and mobile widths covering:
99	  - initial hero and navigation;
100	  - use-case section anchors;
101	  - waitlist invalid and valid states;
102	  - FAQ disclosure;
103	  - responsive layout and no horizontal overflow;
104	  - visible animation/reveal behavior and reduced-motion fallback where the browser runtime supports it.
105	- Report honestly that the waitlist remains a UI-only stub unless a backend is added.

### 51. TOOL RESULT

1527	
1528	### Why only three applications
1529	
1530	#### `apps/web`
1531	
1532	Owns:
1533	
1534	- Next.js routes and UI
1535	- onboarding
1536	- settings editor
1537	- chat and workspace UI
1538	- browser/terminal visualization
1539	- WebSocket subscription
1540	- typed API client usage
1541	
1542	It must not own:
1543	
1544	- task transitions
1545	- authorization decisions
1546	- provider secret handling
1547	- process supervision
1548	- direct SQLite access
1549	- direct calls to Pi
1550	
1551	#### `apps/control-plane`
1552	
1553	Owns central PostgreSQL records only:
1554	
1555	- Google OAuth identity
1556	- account cap
1557	- administrator status
1558	- account-level credits
1559	- container mapping
1560	- runtime registry
1561	- provisioning state
1562	- health summaries
1563	- backup metadata
1564	- admin operations
1565	
1566	It does not own:
1567	
1568	- conversation bodies
1569	- files
1570	- memories
1571	- provider credentials
1572	- browser profiles
1573	- task execution internals
1574	
1575	#### `apps/user-runtime`
1576	
1577	This is the user-container runtime gateway and local authority. It owns:
1578	
1579	- SQLite persistence
1580	- event journal
1581	- task state machine
1582	- approval grants
1583	- leases
1584	- agent processes
1585	- child-task supervision
1586	- providers
1587	- schedules
1588	- memory
1589	- workspace access
1590	- recovery checkpoints
1591	- local WebSocket/HTTP runtime API
1592	
1593	Keep scheduler, supervisor, persistence, and recovery as **modules inside this application initially**, not separate deployables. Split them later only when measurements show a real need.
1594	
1595	That is the key maintainability decision: Dify’s separation teaches us where boundaries matter, but Shera should not pay Dify’s operational cost before the prototype needs it.
1596	
1597	## Feature-module layout
1598	
1599	Inside `apps/user-runtime`:
1600	
1601	```text
1602	apps/user-runtime/src/
1603	├── bootstrap/
1604	├── modules/
1605	│   ├── conversations/
1606	│   ├── tasks/
1607	│   ├── events/
1608	│   ├── approvals/
1609	│   ├── agents/
1610	│   ├── providers/
1611	│   ├── workspace/
1612	│   ├── memory/
1613	│   ├── schedules/
1614	│   ├── recovery/
1615	│   └── runtime/
1616	├── adapters/
1617	│   ├── sqlite/
1618	│   ├── redis/
1619	│   ├── filesystem/
1620	│   ├── browser/
1621	│   ├── process/
1622	│   └── providers/
1623	├── transport/
1624	│   ├── http/
1625	│   ├── websocket/
1626	│   └── local-api/
1627	└── main.ts
1628	```
1629	
1630	Each module should use the same small shape:
1631	
1632	```text
1633	tasks/
1634	├── task.types.ts
1635	├── task.commands.ts
1636	├── task.service.ts
1637	├── task-repository.ts
1638	├── task-transitions.ts
1639	├── task.events.ts
1640	└── task.test.ts
1641	```
1642	
1643	Do not create a package for every file or every domain noun. Keep domain modules together until independent release or deployment is genuinely needed.
1644	
1645	## Shared packages
1646	
1647	### `packages/contracts`
1648	
1649	The only shared source of truth for cross-process and cross-application contracts:
1650	
1651	- HTTP request/response schemas
1652	- WebSocket event envelopes
1653	- Pi JSONL messages
1654	- runtime backend capabilities
1655	- agent-pack manifest schema
1656	- provider configuration schema
1657	- task command schemas
1658	- error response schemas
1659	
1660	Use Zod as the runtime validator and infer TypeScript types from it.
1661	
1662	### `packages/clients`
1663	
1664	Typed clients for:
1665	
1666	- web to control plane
1667	- web to runtime
1668	- control plane to runtime
1669	- runtime session/event subscription
1670	
1671	Follow OpenHands’ discipline: UI code should call typed clients, not construct raw URLs and DTOs throughout the component tree. ([github.com](https://github.com/OpenHands/OpenHands/blob/main/AGENTS.md))
1672	
1673	### `packages/agent-pack-sdk`
1674	
1675	Owns:
1676	
1677	- manifest validation
1678	- pack compatibility checks
1679	- profile loading
1680	- onboarding schema
1681	- settings schema
1682	- UI identity metadata
1683	- capability declarations
1684	- pack version resolution
1685	
1686	It should not own authorization. Pack capabilities are only upper bounds; the runtime policy may narrow them.
1687	
1688	### `packages/pi-protocol`
1689	
1690	Owns:
1691	
1692	- JSONL message schemas
1693	- protocol version negotiation
1694	- correlation IDs
1695	- heartbeat messages
1696	- checkpoint messages
1697	- process failure classification
1698	- fake Pi process for tests
1699	
1700	### `packages/provider-contracts`
1701	
1702	Owns only provider-neutral concepts:
1703	
1704	- model reference
1705	- provider reference
1706	- request/response shape
1707	- usage accounting
1708	- reservation/settlement interfaces
1709	- provider error categories
1710	
1711	Actual OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, Ollama, and OpenAI-compatible adapters belong inside `apps/user-runtime` initially. Do not make every provider a separately published package yet.
1712	
1713	### `packages/observability`
1714	
1715	Owns:
1716	
1717	- structured logging
1718	- redaction
1719	- error-code mapping
1720	- metrics
1721	- correlation IDs
1722	- trace context
1723	- audit/security event helpers
1724	
1725	### `packages/test-kit`
1726	
1727	Owns deterministic fakes:
1728	
1729	- fake provider
1730	- fake Pi process
1731	- fake clock
1732	- fake runtime backend
1733	- fake filesystem
1734	- fake approval responder
1735	- event recorder
1736	- restart/recovery fixture
1737	
1738	This is where OpenHands’ mock-LLM testing approach is most valuable. A fake provider should exercise the real task/runtime path without spending credits or requiring credentials. Its live tests should remain opt-in. ([github.com](https://github.com/OpenHands/OpenHands/blob/main/AGENTS.md))
1739

1765	The pack may declare:

1787	## The maintainability rules to adopt
1788	
1789	### Rule 1: One owner per boundary
1790	
1791	- Web owns rendering.
1792	- Control plane owns central account/runtime operations.
1793	- User runtime owns local execution.
1794	- Pi owns reasoning and tool proposals.
1795	- Pack owns role behavior.
1796	- Adapters own external systems.
1797	
1798	### Rule 2: No raw infrastructure leakage
1799	
1800	- No SQL outside repository adapters.
1801	- No `child_process` outside the supervisor/Pi adapter.
1802	- No provider SDK outside provider adapters.
1803	- No Docker API outside runtime/provisioning adapters.
1804	- No Redis calls inside domain modules.
1805	- No filesystem path assumptions in shared contracts.
1806	
1807	### Rule 3: Typed discriminated unions
1808	
1809	Statuses, event kinds, backend kinds, risk classes, provider types, and agent roles must be literal unions or enums. Never pass arbitrary strings through lifecycle code.
1810	
1811	### Rule 4: Stable error taxonomy
1812	
1813	Every error has:
1814	
1815	```text
1816	code
1817	category
1818	safe_message
1819	retryable
1820	user_action
1821	internal_details
1822	correlation_id
1823	```
1824	
1825	Internal details never cross the user boundary without redaction.
1826	
1827	### Rule 5: State transitions are pure and explicit
1828	
1829	Implement task transitions as a deterministic function:
1830	
1831	```text
1832	(current_state, command, policy, version)
1833	  -> accepted transition | typed rejection
1834	```
1835	
1836	The database transaction then:
1837	
1838	1. checks the expected version
1839	2. applies the transition
1840	3. appends the event
1841	4. updates the projection
1842	5. commits atomically
1843	
1844	### Rule 6: Every side-effecting command is idempotent
1845	
1846	Use:
1847	
1848	```text
1849	command_id
1850	idempotency_key
1851	actor_id
1852	task_id
1853	expected_version
1854	```
1855	
1856	Never deduplicate by message text.
1857	
1858	### Rule 7: Agents propose, runtime decides
1859	
1860	Pi may propose:
1861	
1862	- tool calls
1863	- delegation
1864	- file writes
1865	- external requests
1866	- approval requests
1867	- task completion
1868	
1869	The runtime validates:
1870	
1871	- capability
1872	- grant
1873	- lease
1874	- resource
1875	- budget
1876	- current state
1877	- provider availability
1878	- conflict policy
1879	
1880	### Rule 8: Prefer artifacts over prompt copying
1881	
1882	Large reports, files, logs, and tool results become artifact references. Agents receive metadata and relevant excerpts.
1883	
1884	### Rule 9: Local instructions are scoped
1885	
1886	Use one root `AGENTS.md`, then only add directory-level instructions where ownership or testing rules differ. Agent Zero and OpenHands both show the value of scoped instructions, but copying every directory-level document would increase maintenance cost. ([github.com](https://github.com/agent0ai/agent-zero/blob/main/plugins/_orchestrator/AGENTS.md))
1887	
1888	### Rule 10: Generated files have one source of truth
1889	
1890	Examples:
1891	
1892	- OpenAPI/client types generated from contracts
1893	- Docker Compose generated from templates
1894	- migration metadata generated by the migration tool
1895	- UI translation bundles generated from locale sources
1896	
1897	Dify explicitly marks generated Compose output as non-hand-authored, and OpenHands documents generated client/i18n boundaries. That practice prevents agents from editing the wrong artifact. ([github.com](https://github.com/langgenius/dify/blob/main/web/AGENTS.md))
1898	
1899	## Testing strategy
1900	
1901	### Unit tests
1902	
1903	Mirror the module:
1904	
1905	```text
1906	apps/user-runtime/src/modules/tasks/task-transitions.ts
1907	apps/user-runtime/src/modules/tasks/task-transitions.test.ts
1908	```
1909	
1910	Test:
1911	
1912	- legal and illegal transitions
1913	- approval scope
1914	- retry limits
1915	- idempotency
1916	- lease conflicts
1917	- credit reservation behavior
1918	- pack compatibility
1919	
1920	### Contract tests
1921	
1922	Verify that:
1923	
1924	- web client and control plane agree
1925	- control plane and runtime agree
1926	- runtime and Pi agree
1927	- old event versions are handled correctly
1928	- unknown fields are rejected where required
1929	
1930	### Integration tests
1931	
1932	Run with:
1933	
1934	- real SQLite
1935	- real migrations
1936	- fake Pi
1937	- fake provider
1938	- fake clock
1939	- isolated temporary filesystem
1940	
1941	### Recovery tests
1942	
1943	Test:
1944	
1945	- process death
1946	- gateway restart
1947	- interrupted tool
1948	- pending approval
1949	- queued task
1950	- Redis unavailable
1951	- provider timeout
1952	- ambiguous provider response
1953	- duplicate webhook
1954	- browser reconnect
1955	
1956	### E2E tests
1957	
1958	Use three modes:
1959	
1960	```text
1961	mock-runtime       fast deterministic UI and workflow tests
1962	local-runtime      real gateway + fake provider/Pi
1963	live-provider      opt-in, expensive, never default CI
1964	```
1965	
1966	OpenHands’ separation of mock-LLM E2E and live LLM E2E is a strong pattern to adopt. ([github.com](https://github.com/OpenHands/OpenHands/blob/main/AGENTS.md))
1967

2071	## Revised recommendation
2072	
2073	The best architecture is:
2074	
2075	```text
2076	                    ┌─────────────┐
2077	                    │  Next.js UI │
2078	                    └──────┬──────┘
2079	                           │ typed client
2080	                    ┌──────▼──────┐
2081	                    │Control Plane│
2082	                    │ auth/credits│
2083	                    │runtime map  │
2084	                    └──────┬──────┘
2085	                           │ runtime contract
2086	                 ┌─────────▼─────────┐
2087	                 │ User Runtime       │
2088	                 │ gateway + SQLite   │
2089	                 │ task/event/approval│
2090	                 │ supervisor         │
2091	                 └───────┬───────────┘
2092	                         │ JSONL
2093	          ┌──────────────┼──────────────┐
2094	          │              │              │
2095	   Researcher Pi   Developer Pi   General Pi
2096	          │              │              │
2097	       child tasks, tools, workspace, providers
2098	```
2099	
2100	Cloud is an adapter behind the same runtime contract:
2101	
2102	```text
2103	LocalContainerBackend
2104	RemoteContainerBackend
2105	ManagedCloudBackend
2106	```
2107	
2108	The browser never knows whether the selected runtime is local, remote, or managed. This follows OpenHands’ backend-registry and cloud-sandbox boundary while preserving Shera’s local-first design. ([github.com](https://github.com/OpenHands/docs/blob/main/openhands/usage/agent-canvas/overview.mdx))
2109	
2110	## Grilling decision
2111	
2112	The previous “strict monorepo with many independent packages” proposal is replaced with this more maintainable strategy:
2113	
2114	- three applications
2115	- seven shared packages maximum initially
2116	- feature modules inside applications
2117	- one student agent pack first
2118	- runtime services remain modules until scale proves they need to split
2119	- typed contracts and generated clients at process boundaries
2120	- mock-first testing
2121	- cloud behind a backend registry
2122

2128	===== [76] ASSISTANT @ 2026-09-11T03:50:40.295Z =====
2129	Baseline accepted. The next decision is the database access layer because Shera uses SQLite locally and PostgreSQL centrally, and we want future migration without hiding important SQL behavior. I’m checking the current capabilities of the main TypeScript options before recommending one.

2131	===== [77] ASSISTANT @ 2026-09-11T03:51:12.542Z =====
2132	**Question 24: Database toolkit**
2133	
2134	For Shera’s two deliberately different databases:
2135	
2136	- central PostgreSQL for accounts, credits, runtime registry, and administration
2137	- container-local SQLite for conversations, tasks, events, approvals, memory, and recovery
2138	
2139	I recommend **Drizzle ORM with separate schemas and migration histories**.
2140	
2141	### Why Drizzle fits Shera
2142	
2143	Drizzle supports both SQLite and PostgreSQL, uses TypeScript schema definitions, and produces reviewable SQL migrations. It also supports separate configuration and migration directories for different databases. ([orm.drizzle.team](https://orm.drizzle.team/docs/get-started/node-sqlite-existing?utm_source=openai))
2144	
2145	That gives us:
2146	
2147	- explicit SQL rather than a heavily abstracted persistence layer
2148	- TypeScript inference from schema definitions
2149	- migrations that humans and coding agents can inspect
2150	- independent SQLite and PostgreSQL schemas
2151	- relatively small runtime overhead
2152	- fewer generated-client artifacts than Prisma
2153	- straightforward repository adapters
2154	
2155	### Why not one portable schema
2156	
2157	We should not attempt to share one physical schema between SQLite and PostgreSQL. They serve different domains and have different constraints.
2158	
2159	```text
2160	apps/control-plane/src/db/
2161	├── schema/
2162	│   ├── users.ts
2163	│   ├── credits.ts
2164	│   ├── runtimes.ts
2165	│   └── backups.ts
2166	├── migrations/
2167	├── client.ts
2168	└── drizzle.config.ts
2169	```
2170	
2171	```text
2172	apps/user-runtime/src/db/
2173	├── schema/
2174	│   ├── conversations.ts
2175	│   ├── tasks.ts
2176	│   ├── events.ts
2177	│   ├── approvals.ts
2178	│   ├── memories.ts
2179	│   └── schedules.ts
2180	├── migrations/
2181	├── pragmas.ts
2182	├── client.ts
2183	└── drizzle.config.ts
2184	```
2185	
2186	Prisma supports SQLite and PostgreSQL, but its migration files are provider-specific, and switching database providers requires separate migration histories. Its multiple-database setup also introduces separate generated clients and configuration. That is workable, but less attractive for this small, dual-database architecture. ([prisma.io](https://www.prisma.io/docs/guides/database/multiple-databases?utm_source=openai))
2187	
2188	Kysely is a strong alternative when maximum SQL control is the priority. It supports multiple dialects, including SQLite and PostgreSQL. However, Shera would need more separate schema and migration tooling, increasing the number of conventions that you and agents must maintain. ([kysely-org.github.io](https://kysely-org.github.io/kysely-apidoc/interfaces/Dialect.html?utm_source=openai))
2189	
2190	### Required database rules
2191	
2192	- Never use schema push in deployed environments.
2193	- Generate and commit migrations.
2194	- Review generated SQL before applying it.
2195	- Test every migration from the previous released database version.
2196	- Test backup restoration followed by migration.
2197	- Enable SQLite foreign keys and WAL mode explicitly.
2198	- Run SQLite integrity checks during backup verification.
2199	- Keep transactions short.
2200	- No database calls from React components or Pi processes.
2201	- No raw SQL outside database adapters, migrations, and explicitly reviewed performance queries.
2202	- Repository interfaces remain domain-oriented.
2203	- Every state transition and its event append occur in one transaction.
2204	- Never serialize executable data or use `eval`.
2205	- Central and local migration versions are tracked independently.
2206	
2207	### Recommendation
2208	
2209	Use:
2210	
2211	```text
2212	Drizzle ORM
2213	PostgreSQL driver: node-postgres
2214	SQLite driver: node:sqlite initially, subject to the runtime spike
2215	Separate migration directories
2216	Separate schema definitions
2217	Repository interfaces above Drizzle
2218	```
2219	
2220	Drizzle currently documents native SQLite support through `node:sqlite`, `better-sqlite3`, and libSQL, as well as PostgreSQL through `node-postgres` and `postgres.js`. ([orm.drizzle.team](https://orm.drizzle.team/docs/sqlite/get-started-sqlite?utm_source=openai))

2233	**Question 25: API contract strategy**
2234	
2235	How should the web app, control plane, runtime gateway, and Pi adapter share contracts?
2236	
2237	1. **Zod-first contracts, recommended:** define request, response, command, and event schemas in `packages/contracts`; derive TypeScript types and generate OpenAPI documentation/clients from those schemas.
2238	2. **OpenAPI-first:** write an OpenAPI document manually and generate server/client types from it.
2239	3. **TypeScript types only:** share interfaces, but validate payloads manually at runtime.
2240	4. **tRPC-style end-to-end RPC:** use a single TypeScript RPC framework between applications.
2241

2243	
2244	### Why this is easiest to maintain
2245	
2246	A Zod-first contract gives agents one obvious place to look:
2247	
2248	```text
2249	packages/contracts/
2250	├── http/
2251	├── events/
2252	├── commands/
2253	├── pi/
2254	├── runtime/
2255	├── providers/
2256	├── packs/
2257	└── errors/

2259	
2260	Each boundary gets both:

2264	runtimeStatusSchema.parse(payload)
2265	type RuntimeStatus = z.infer<typeof runtimeStatusSchema>

2267	
2268	The same schemas can support:
2269	
2270	- Express request validation
2271	- WebSocket event validation
2272	- JSONL Pi messages

2280	
2281	```text
2282	packages/contracts
2283	        │
2284	        ├── web client
2285	        ├── control-plane API
2286	        ├── runtime gateway
2287	        ├── Pi adapter
2288	        └── test harness

2290	
2291	Rules:
2292	
2293	- Contracts contain schemas and types, not database queries or business logic.
2294	- Every event has a version.
2295	- Unknown fields are rejected for internal protocol messages unless explicitly marked extensible.
2296	- User-facing HTTP errors use stable error codes.
2297	- WebSocket and Pi messages carry correlation IDs.

2299	- Runtime and control-plane versions negotiate compatibility.
