2315	**Question 26: Real-time event delivery**
2316	
2317	How should WebSockets work?
2318	
2319	1. **Live-only WebSocket:** events are pushed while connected; the client reloads all history after reconnect.
2320	2. **Durable event journal plus replay cursor, recommended:** WebSocket delivers live events, while the runtime stores events durably and replays from the client’s last acknowledged cursor.
2321	3. **Redis-only event stream:** Redis Streams are the source of truth for live and missed events.
2322	4. **Polling plus occasional streaming:** use HTTP polling for state and WebSockets only for visual updates.
2323	
2324	I recommend **option 2**.
2325	
2326	### Event flow
2327	
2328	```text
2329	command request
2330	      │
2331	      ▼
2332	runtime transaction
2333	  ├── update current state
2334	  ├── append durable event
2335	  └── commit
2336	      │
2337	      ├── WebSocket fanout
2338	      └── replay availability
2339	```
2340	
2341	Each event should contain:
2342	
2343	```text
2344	event_id
2345	stream_id
2346	sequence
2347	event_type
2348	event_version
2349	actor
2350	task_id
2351	conversation_id
2352	occurred_at
2353	payload
2354	redaction_class
2355	```
2356	
2357	Each client maintains:
2358	
2359	```text
2360	stream_id
2361	last_received_sequence
2362	last_acknowledged_sequence
2363	connection_id
2364	```
2365	
2366	Reconnect behavior:
2367	
2368	1. Client sends its last acknowledged cursor.
2369	2. Runtime replays missing durable events.
2370	3. Runtime resumes live delivery.
2371	4. Client marks events as replayed or live.
2372	5. Duplicate event IDs are ignored.
2373	6. If the cursor is outside retention, the runtime returns a snapshot plus a new cursor.
2374	
2375	### Why Redis should not be the source of truth
2376	
2377	Redis is useful for fanout, transient queues, locks, and wakeups. It should not be the only record of chat messages, approvals, task transitions, or recovery events. Dify’s queue/event design is useful here because it separates event semantics from transport details. ([github.com](https://github.com/langgenius/dify/blob/main/api/core/app/apps/base_app_queue_manager.py))
2378	
2379	### Maintenance rules
2380	
2381	- The event journal is append-only.
2382	- Event payloads are immutable.
2383	- Projections can be rebuilt.
2384	- Every event type has a schema and version.
2385	- WebSocket handlers do not directly mutate task state.
2386	- Commands use HTTP or a typed command channel.
2387	- Events are emitted only after the durable transaction succeeds.
2388	- Heartbeats are transport-level and are not business events.
2389	- A disconnected client never causes task cancellation.
2390	- Browser closure does not stop work.
2391	
2392	Do you accept **durable event journal plus replay cursors, with WebSockets used for live fanout**?

2402	===== [86] ASSISTANT @ 2026-09-11T04:02:59.212Z =====
2403	**Question 27: Execution isolation**
2404	
2405	How should terminal, code execution, browser automation, and agent-created tools be isolated in the prototype?
2406	
2407	1. **Container boundary plus restricted child processes, recommended for version one:** the user container is the primary isolation boundary; child tasks run as an unprivileged user with path, resource, timeout, and network policies.
2408	2. **Nested container per task:** every task runs in its own Docker container.
2409	3. **Dedicated sandbox service:** use a separate sandbox runtime such as a remote execution service or microVM layer.
2410	4. **No sandbox distinction:** tools execute directly as the agent process.
2411	
2412	I recommend **option 1 for the prototype**, with a clear security boundary:
2413	
2414	### Version-one isolation
2415	
2416	- One user container is isolated from other users and the host.
2417	- Pi processes run without host Docker socket access.
2418	- Child tasks run under a restricted OS user.
2419	- Workspace paths are resolved and checked before every file operation.
2420	- Working directory is fixed to the task/workspace scope.
2421	- Resource limits apply to CPU, memory, process count, disk usage, and wall-clock time.
2422	- Network access is disabled by default for code execution.
2423	- Network access is capability-based for research/browser/provider operations.
2424	- External writes remain approval-gated.
2425	- Tool-created processes inherit the parent task’s limits.
2426	- Process trees are terminated on cancellation.
2427	- Logs and errors are redacted.
2428	
2429	### Important limitation
2430	
2431	A restricted child process inside the same user container is not equivalent to a hardened microVM or separate task container. The container is the primary security boundary in version one. A later runtime backend can provide stronger isolation without changing the agent/task contracts.
2432	
2433	### Why not nested Docker immediately
2434	
2435	Nested Docker would conflict with:
2436	
2437	- the PRD’s prohibition on host Docker socket access
2438	- simple local development
2439	- portability to managed cloud backends
2440	- low operational overhead
2441	
2442	This is also where OpenHands’ distinction between “direct host operation” and “Docker sandbox mode” is useful: the product must make the security mode visible and never imply that host access is safe by default. ([github.com](https://github.com/OpenHands/OpenHands/blob/main/README.md))
2443	
2444	### Required security tests
2445	
2446	- path traversal and symlink escape
2447	- workspace boundary escape
2448	- environment-variable secret exposure
2449	- process-tree escape
2450	- resource exhaustion
2451	- network policy bypass
2452	- credential leakage in stdout/stderr
2453	- agent-created tool privilege escalation
2454	- cross-task lease violation
2455	- cross-user container access
2456	
2457	Do you accept **container boundary plus restricted child processes for version one**, with stronger sandbox backends added later?
2458

2468	**Question 28: Agent-pack distribution**
2469	
2470	How should agent packs be delivered?
2471	
2472	1. **Built into the Shera monorepo, recommended for version one:** packs are versioned directories in the repository and selected from a local registry.
2473	2. **Separate package repositories:** every customer pack is independently published and installed.
2474	3. **User-uploaded packs:** users or admins upload pack archives through the UI.
2475	4. **Marketplace first:** packs are downloaded dynamically from a remote registry.
2476	
2477	I recommend **option 1 for the prototype**, with a registry boundary that can later load signed external packs.
2478	
2479	### Version-one approach
2480	
2481	```text
2482	agent-packs/
2483	├── student/
2484	│   ├── v1/
2485	│   └── v2/
2486	└── registry.ts
2487	```
2488	
2489	Each workspace stores:
2490	
2491	```text
2492	agent_pack_id
2493	agent_pack_version
2494	manifest_hash
2495	installed_at
2496	```
2497	
2498	When a task starts, it stores a pack snapshot:
2499	
2500	```text
2501	task_agent_pack_id
2502	task_agent_pack_version
2503	task_agent_pack_hash
2504	```
2505	
2506	This ensures that a pack upgrade cannot silently change an existing task.
2507	
2508	### Why this is easiest to maintain
2509	
2510	- You can change the student pack without changing the runtime.
2511	- Agents can inspect one consistent pack layout.
2512	- Pack review happens in the same pull request as runtime changes.
2513	- CI can validate every manifest and profile.
2514	- No package registry, remote download, or signature service is required initially.
2515	- External packs can later implement the same `AgentPackLoader` interface.
2516	
2517	### Future external-pack boundary
2518	
2519	```text
2520	interface AgentPackLoader {
2521	  resolve(packId, version): Promise<AgentPack>
2522	  validate(manifest): ValidationResult
2523	  verifyIntegrity(pack): IntegrityResult
2524	  checkCompatibility(pack, runtimeVersion): CompatibilityResult
2525	}
2526	```
2527	
2528	Future loaders may support:
2529	
2530	```text
2531	BuiltInPackLoader
2532	SignedPackagePackLoader
2533	AdminRegistryPackLoader
2534	```
2535	
2536	### Trust rules
2537	
2538	- A pack is data and configuration, not unrestricted code.
2539	- Pack prompts cannot bypass authorization.
2540	- Pack capabilities are upper bounds.
2541	- Tool permissions are rechecked by the runtime.
2542	- Pack manifests are schema-validated.
2543	- Pack hashes are recorded.
2544	- Pack changes require compatibility checks.
2545	- Existing tasks remain pinned to the original version.
2546	- Packs cannot contain secrets, host paths, Docker socket access, or arbitrary startup commands.
2547	
2548	This takes Agent Zero’s profile composition and OpenHands’ explicit repository-boundary discipline without introducing marketplace complexity too early. ([github.com](https://github.com/agent0ai/agent-zero/blob/main/agents/AGENTS.md))

2568	## Revised product model
2569	
2570	```text
2571	Customer Pack
2572	└── Scenes
2573	    ├── Expert Teams
2574	    │   └── Experts
2575	    ├── Individual Experts
2576	    ├── Skills
2577	    └── Connectors

2579	
2580	### 1. Customer Pack
2581	
2582	A fixed audience-specific set of capabilities.
2583	
2584	Examples:
2585	
2586	```text
2587	Student Pack
2588	Developer Pack
2589	Research Lab Pack
2590	Small Business Pack
2591	Content Creator Pack
2592	```
2593	
2594	The first version can ship with:
2595	
2596	```text
2597	Student Pack
2598	```
2599	
2600	The runtime does not know student-specific behavior. It only knows how to load and execute a valid pack.
2601	
2602	### 2. Scene
2603	
2604	A task-oriented working environment inside a pack.
2605	
2606	Examples for the Student Pack:
2607	
2608	```text
2609	Exam Preparation
2610	Research Paper
2611	Literature Review
2612	Programming Assignment
2613	Data Analysis
2614	Presentation Preparation
2615	Career Preparation
2616	```
2617	
2618	A scene defines:
2619	
2620	- its purpose
2621	- recommended experts
2622	- optional expert team
2623	- default skills
2624	- allowed connectors
2625	- default model/provider policy
2626	- collaboration rules
2627	- onboarding questions
2628	- output templates
2629	- approval defaults
2630	- UI metadata
2631	
2632	A scene is not a new runtime. It is a declarative task configuration.
2633	
2634	### 3. Expert
2635	
2636	A specialized agent with a stable identity and role.
2637	
2638	Examples:
2639	
2640	```text
2641	Researcher
2642	Source Verifier
2643	Citation Manager
2644	Programming Tutor
2645	Code Reviewer
2646	Data Analyst
2647	Writing Coach
2648	Presentation Coach
2649	Study Planner
2650	```
2651	
2652	Experts can be reused across multiple scenes. For example, `Source Verifier` may belong to both the `Research Paper` and `Literature Review` scenes.
2653	
2654	### 4. Expert Team
2655	
2656	A predefined collaboration group made from experts.
2657

2664	├── Citation Manager

2902	## Revised product model
2903	
2904	The primary user experience is a Telegram-like interface:
2905	
2906	```text
2907	┌──────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────┐
2908	│ Sidebar              │ Main conversation                    │
2909	│                      │                                     │
2910	│ Experts              │ Chat with selected expert            │
2911	│  Researcher          │                                     │
2912	│  Developer           │ Messages, files, tools, progress     │
2913	│  Citation Manager    │ approvals, artifacts, task status    │
2914	│                      │                                     │
2915	│ Operations           │                                     │
2916	│  Research Paper      │                                     │
2917	│  Exam Preparation    │                                     │
2918	│  Programming Task   │                                     │
2919	└──────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────┘
2920	```
2921	
2922	### Expert chats
2923	
2924	An expert is a persistent, reusable agent identity.
2925	
2926	Examples:
2927	
2928	```text
2929	Researcher
2930	Developer
2931	Citation Manager
2932	Programming Tutor
2933	Data Analyst
2934	Writing Coach
2935	```
2936	
2937	When the user opens an expert from the sidebar, they get a direct chat with that expert.
2938	
2939	An expert can:
2940	
2941	- answer independently
2942	- inspect shared workspace files
2943	- use its permitted skills and connectors
2944	- create or continue tasks
2945	- recommend an Operation
2946	- ask another expert to help
2947	- publish artifacts
2948	- join an existing Operation
2949	- report progress and uncertainty
2950	
2951	The expert is not restricted to one Operation. A `Researcher` can participate in:
2952	
2953	```text
2954	Research Paper
2955	Exam Preparation
2956	Data Analysis
2957	Presentation Preparation
2958	```
2959	
2960	The expert’s identity, profile, private memory, and direct conversation remain persistent.
2961	
2962	### Operations
2963	
2964	An Operation replaces the previous term “Scene.”
2965	
2966	An Operation is a reusable task pattern and collaborative working context.
2967	
2968	Examples:
2969	
2970	```text
2971	Research Paper
2972	Exam Preparation
2973	Programming Assignment
2974	Data Analysis
2975	Presentation Preparation
2976	Career Preparation
2977	```
2978	
2979	When the user opens an Operation, Shera creates an Operation chat containing:
2980	
2981	- the user
2982	- General Assistant or orchestrator
2983	- recommended experts
2984	- optional additional experts
2985	- shared task context
2986	- operation-specific skills
2987	- operation-specific connectors
2988	- operation-specific output expectations
2989	
2990	The agents can talk to each other transparently inside that chat:
2991	
2992	```text

2994	  ↕
2995	General Assistant
2996	  ↕
2997	Researcher
2998	  ↕
2999	Citation Manager

### 53. TOOL RESULT

3000	  ↕
3001	Writing Coach
3002	```
3003	
3004	The Operation is not a new class of agent. It is a collaboration configuration that selects and coordinates existing experts.
3005	
3006	### Correct hierarchy
3007

3009	Customer Pack
3010	├── Experts
3011	├── Expert Teams
3012	├── Operations

3325	===== [101] ASSISTANT @ 2026-09-11T04:28:36.602Z =====
3326	## Question 32: Expert process lifecycle
3327	
3328	How should expert processes behave now that a pack may contain many specialized experts?
3329	
3330	1. **Every expert stays running:** each configured expert gets a permanent Pi process.
3331	2. **Persistent identity, on-demand process, recommended:** expert profiles, chats, memory, and state persist, but Pi processes start when needed and hibernate or stop when idle.
3332	3. **Shared process per pack:** all experts in a pack share one Pi process.
3333	4. **One process per Operation:** all experts collaborating in an Operation share a single process.
3334	
3335	I recommend **option 2**, revising the earlier process decision.
3336	
3337	### Persistent expert identity
3338	
3339	The following remains durable even when no process is running:
3340	
3341	```text
3342	expert_id
3343	pack_id
3344	pack_version
3345	profile
3346	direct_chat
3347	private_memory
3348	settings
3349	capabilities
3350	last_checkpoint
3351	current_assignments
3352	```
3353	
3354	### On-demand execution
3355	
3356	```text
3357	User messages Researcher
3358	        │
3359	        ▼
3360	Runtime loads Researcher profile and checkpoint
3361	        │
3362	        ▼
3363	Runtime starts or reuses a supervised Pi process
3364	        │
3365	        ▼
3366	Expert handles the message or task
3367	        │
3368	        ▼
3369	State and checkpoint are persisted
3370	        │
3371	        ▼
3372	Process remains warm briefly, then hibernates
3373	```
3374	
3375	### Recommended process policy
3376	
3377	- Keep an expert process warm while it has an active conversation or Operation task.
3378	- Reuse it for related work when safe.
3379	- Stop it after a configurable idle timeout.
3380	- Restore from durable state when needed again.
3381	- Operation task execution still uses supervised child processes.
3382	- Resource pressure may terminate idle expert processes immediately.
3383	- Process termination must not erase chat, memory, task, or identity.
3384	- No expert process is trusted as the sole holder of durable state.
3385	
3386	### Why this is better
3387	
3388	For example, the Student Pack might contain ten experts, but a particular user may only be using:
3389	
3390	```text
3391	General Assistant
3392	Researcher
3393	Citation Manager
3394	```
3395	
3396	Only those processes need to be active.
3397	
3398	This also supports future packs without multiplying permanent memory usage:
3399	
3400	```text
3401	Student Pack: 10 experts
3402	Developer Pack: 8 experts
3403	Business Pack: 12 experts
3404	```
3405	
3406	Thirty declared experts would still result in only the currently active processes running.
3407	
3408	### Revised earlier decision
3409	
3410	Replace:
3411	
3412	> One long-lived process per role.
3413	
3414	With:
3415	
3416	> Each expert has a persistent logical identity and resumable state. Its Pi process is supervised, started on demand, kept warm while active, and safely stopped when idle. Actual task work may use separately supervised child processes.
3417	
3418	Do you accept this revision?
3419

3428	===== [104] ASSISTANT @ 2026-09-11T04:31:02.175Z =====
3429	## Decision 32 refined
3430	
3431	Use three separate lifecycle concepts:
3432	
3433	### 1. Persistent expert identity
3434	
3435	Always durable:
3436	
3437	```text
3438	expert profile
3439	direct chat
3440	private memory
3441	settings
3442	capabilities
3443	operation memberships
3444	task history
3445	```
3446	
3447	This survives process termination, container restart, pack upgrade, and browser closure.
3448	
3449	### 2. Warm expert session
3450	
3451	A supervised Pi process used for conversation, planning, and coordination.
3452	
3453	- Starts on demand.
3454	- Remains warm while actively used.
3455	- May hibernate after an idle timeout.
3456	- Can be reconstructed from durable chat, memory, and checkpoint state.
3457	- Is not the sole owner of any durable information.
3458	
3459	### 3. Persistent task worker
3460	
3461	A separately supervised process for actual work.
3462	
3463	If a task is:
3464	
3465	```text
3466	RUNNING
3467	WAITING_FOR_TOOL
3468	WAITING_FOR_AGENT
3469	WAITING_FOR_APPROVAL
3470	RETRYING
3471	CANCELLING
3472	```
3473	
3474	its lifecycle is governed by the task, not the chat’s idle timer.
3475	
3476	Rules:
3477	
3478	- A running worker is never stopped by the expert-session idle timeout.
3479	- Closing the browser does not stop the task.
3480	- The worker sends heartbeats.
3481	- The runtime gateway supervises the process tree.
3482	- Progress and checkpoints are written to SQLite.
3483	- The task lease is renewed while the worker remains healthy.
3484	- A stalled worker is detected separately from a slow but healthy worker.
3485	- Resource pressure may pause or queue new work, but should not kill healthy running work arbitrarily.
3486	- Cancellation terminates the worker and its descendants.
3487	- Scheduled Operations may wake the required experts and workers automatically.
3488	
3489	## Long-running job model
3490	
3491	```text
3492	Expert session
3493	    │
3494	    ├── proposes task
3495	    │
3496	    ▼
3497	Durable task record
3498	    │
3499	    ▼
3500	Supervised task worker
3501	    │
3502	    ├── heartbeat
3503	    ├── progress events
3504	    ├── checkpoints
3505	    ├── artifacts
3506	    └── final result
3507	```
3508	
3509	The user can leave and return later:
3510	
3511	```text
3512	User closes browser
3513	        │
3514	        ▼
3515	Task continues
3516	        │
3517	        ├── progress saved
3518	        ├── events journaled
3519	        └── artifacts persisted
3520	        │
3521	        ▼
3522	User reconnects
3523	        │
3524	        ├── missed events replayed
3525	        └── current status displayed
3526	```
3527	
3528	## Process or container failure
3529	
3530	The task itself remains durable.
3531	
3532	After restart:
3533	
3534	1. Runtime finds tasks that were not terminal.
3535	2. Runtime inspects the last checkpoint and last known side effect.
3536	3. Runtime determines whether continuation is safe.
3537	4. Safe work resumes automatically.
3538	5. Work with an uncertain external effect pauses for review.
3539	6. The user sees `RECOVERING`, `RESUMED`, or `RECOVERY_REQUIRED`.
3540	
3541	A task should not claim “resume from exactly where it stopped” unless the tool and task step actually support that. Some work will resume from a safe checkpoint or repeat an idempotent step.
3542	
3543	## Recommended policy
3544	
3545	```text
3546	Idle expert, no active task
3547	→ process may hibernate
3548	
3549	Expert coordinating an active Operation
3550	→ keep warm when resources permit
3551	
3552	Healthy long-running task
3553	→ keep worker alive
3554	
3555	Worker crash with safe checkpoint
3556	→ restart and resume
3557	
3558	Container restart with safe checkpoint
3559	→ restore and resume
3560	
3561	Unknown external side effect
3562	→ pause for review
3563	
3564	User cancellation
3565	→ terminate complete process tree
3566	```
3567	
3568	This is the best user-facing balance: long-running work persists, but unused experts do not consume resources forever.
3569	
3570	## Question 33: Checkpoint granularity
3571	
3572	For recovery, I recommend checkpointing at **safe semantic boundaries**, not after every token or arbitrary timer.
3573	
3574	Examples:
3575	
3576	```text
3577	search query completed
3578	document downloaded
3579	source set finalized
3580	analysis artifact saved
3581	code patch applied
3582	test command completed
3583	approval received
3584	external request confirmed
3585	```
3586	
3587	Each checkpoint records:
3588	
3589	```text
3590	task_id
3591	step_id
3592	checkpoint_version
3593	completed_actions
3594	pending_action
3595	artifact_refs
3596	provider_usage
3597	lease_state
3598	approval_state

3615	## Question 34: Chat messages and task runs
3616	
3617	How should a user message become executable work?
3618	
3619	1. **Every message is a task:** simple internal model, but ordinary conversation creates excessive task records.
3620	2. **Messages and tasks are separate, recommended:** every message is persisted as conversation content; only messages that trigger tools, delegation, autonomy, approvals, or long-running work create a task run.
3621	3. **One task per chat:** all messages in an expert or Operation chat belong to one continuously running task.
3622	4. **User explicitly starts tasks:** normal messages are conversational until the user clicks “Run as task.”
3623	
3624	I recommend **option 2**.
3625	
3626	### Example: direct expert chat
3627	
3628	```text
3629	User: "What is opportunity cost?"
3630	→ conversation message
3631	→ normal model turn
3632	→ no durable task run required
3633	```
3634	
3635	```text
3636	User: "Read these PDFs and compare the theories."
3637	→ conversation message
3638	→ task run created
3639	→ tools, artifacts, events, checkpoints, and credits tracked
3640	```
3641	
3642	### Example: Operation chat
3643	
3644	```text
3645	Operation workspace: Research Paper
3646	
3647	Task 1: Find relevant sources
3648	Task 2: Compare theoretical frameworks
3649	Task 3: Draft literature review
3650	Task 4: Check citations
3651	```
3652	
3653	Each task is linked to:
3654	
3655	```text
3656	conversation_id
3657	operation_workspace_id
3658	parent_task_id
3659	expert_roster
3660	provider_snapshot
3661	pack_version
3662	operation_version
3663	```
3664	
3665	### Benefits
3666	
3667	- Normal conversation remains lightweight.
3668	- Tool-running work gets full lifecycle handling.
3669	- Credits are charged only when model/tool execution requires it.
3670	- Approvals attach to a concrete task and action.
3671	- Long-running work can continue after the chat is closed.
3672	- Multiple tasks can exist in one Operation workspace without corrupting one task’s history.
3673	- The user still sees a seamless chat timeline.
3674	
3675	Internally, the runtime distinguishes:
3676	
3677	```text
3678	Conversation
3679	Message
3680	Turn
3681	TaskRun
3682	ToolExecution
3683	Checkpoint
3684	Artifact
3685	```
3686	
3687	A `Turn` represents one response cycle. A `TaskRun` represents work that needs lifecycle supervision.
3688

3810	## Question 35: Tool execution in direct expert chats

3838	## Decision 35 recorded and clarified
3839	
3840	### Default behavior
3841	
3842	```text
3843	Read-only tool
3844	→ execute automatically
3845	
3846	Local file mutation
3847	→ show preview and request approval
3848	
3849	Code execution
3850	→ show command, working directory, resource policy, and approval state
3851	
3852	Repository mutation
3853	→ show diff/branch/worktree and request approval
3854	
3855	External write
3856	→ request approval
3857	
3858	Destructive or irreversible action
3859	→ request explicit approval
3860	```
3861	
3862	### Reusable grants
3863	
3864	A user may configure a grant such as:
3865	
3866	```text
3867	Always approve this tool
3868	Always approve this tool for this expert
3869	Always approve this tool in this workspace
3870	Always approve this tool for this repository/path
3871	```
3872	
3873	If the requested action matches the grant exactly, the runtime executes it without another prompt.
3874	
3875	The grant must still satisfy:
3876	
3877	- correct user
3878	- correct expert
3879	- correct workspace
3880	- correct tool version
3881	- correct capability
3882	- correct resource scope
3883	- correct operation/task policy
3884	- not expired
3885	- not revoked
3886	- not broader than the requested action
3887	
3888	### What “full access” means
3889	
3890	“Full access” is not a hidden agent mode. It is a set of explicit capability grants.
3891	
3892	For example:
3893	
3894	```text
3895	Developer
3896	├── read workspace: allowed
3897	├── write project-x: allowed
3898	├── run local code: allowed
3899	├── external network: denied
3900	├── deploy production: denied
3901	└── send external message: denied
3902	```
3903	
3904	The user can grant more access through settings, but the runtime still enforces:
3905	
3906	- tenant boundary
3907	- container boundary
3908	- path boundary
3909	- resource limits
3910	- tool capability
3911	- credential policy
3912	- pack restrictions
3913	- task state
3914	- provider and credit policy
3915	
3916	A prompt cannot override those rules.

3918	## Question 36: Who may add experts to an Operation?
3919	
3920	When an Operation is running, who may change the expert roster?

3923	2. **User and General Assistant, recommended:** the user can change the roster directly; General Assistant may propose additions or removals, which the runtime applies according to the Operation policy.
3924	3. **Any participating expert:** any expert may invite or remove another expert.
3925	4. **Fixed roster:** the roster cannot change after the Operation starts.
3926

3974	## Question 37: Private and shared memory
3975	
3976	How should expert memory work when experts collaborate in an Operation?
3977	
3978	1. **Everything shared:** all participating experts can read one another’s private memory.
3979	2. **Private by default, explicit publication, recommended:** private memory stays private; experts publish selected notes, facts, decisions, or artifacts into the Operation’s shared context.
3980	3. **Operation-only memory:** experts have no private memory during Operations.
3981	4. **General Assistant controls all memory:** experts submit memory candidates to General Assistant, which decides what becomes shared.
3982	
3983	I recommend **option 2**.
3984	
3985	### Memory scopes
3986	
3987	```text
3988	Account memory
3989	Workspace memory
3990	Operation memory
3991	Task memory
3992	Expert-private memory
3993	```
3994	
3995	### Default visibility
3996	
3997	| Memory type | Visible to |
3998	|---|---|
3999	| Expert-private memory | That expert only |
4000	| Direct-chat memory | That expert and user |
4001	| Operation memory | User and participating experts |
4002	| Task memory | Task participants and runtime |
4003	| Workspace memory | Agents allowed by workspace policy |
4004	| Account memory | User and explicitly authorized runtime services |
4005	
4006	### Explicit publication
4007	
4008	An expert may propose:
4009	
4010	```text
4011	MemoryCandidateProposed
4012	```
4013	
4014	with:
4015	
4016	```text
4017	memory_id
4018	scope
4019	summary
4020	source_refs
4021	confidence
4022	sensitivity
4023	expires_at
4024	```
4025	
4026	The runtime applies policy:
4027	
4028	- public-to-operation notes may be shared automatically
4029	- private memories cannot be copied automatically
4030	- sensitive memories require user approval
4031	- credentials and secrets are rejected
4032	- source references are preserved
4033	- the user can inspect, edit, revoke, or forget memory
4034	- memory provenance is retained
4035	- pack upgrades do not silently rewrite existing memory
4036	
4037	### Example
4038	
4039	```text
4040	Researcher private memory:
4041	"User prefers Scandinavian economic history sources."
4042	→ stays private
4043	
4044	Researcher publishes to Operation:
4045	"Use peer-reviewed sources from 2015 onward."
4046	→ available to the Operation team
4047	
4048	Citation Manager:
4049	"Source index contains 12 verified papers."
4050	→ available to the Operation team
4051	```
4052	
4053	This preserves useful collaboration while respecting expert privacy and making information flow visible.
4054

4066	## Question 38: Skills, tools, and connectors
4067	
4068	I recommend treating them as three separate layers:
4069	
4070	```text
4071	Skill
4072	→ instructions and workflow knowledge
4073	
4074	Tool
4075	→ executable capability
4076	
4077	Connector
4078	→ authenticated integration with an external system
4079	```
4080	
4081	### Skill
4082	
4083	A skill teaches an expert how to perform a kind of work.
4084	
4085	Examples:
4086	
4087	```text
4088	Literature Review
4089	Exam Revision Plan
4090	Citation Checking
4091	Debugging Workflow
4092	Spreadsheet Analysis
4093	Presentation Outline
4094	```
4095	
4096	A skill may contain:
4097	
4098	- instructions
4099	- templates
4100	- examples
4101	- expected outputs
4102	- quality checks
4103	- recommended tools
4104	- compatible expert roles
4105	
4106	A skill cannot execute code, access credentials, or grant permission.
4107	
4108	### Tool
4109	
4110	A tool performs an action.
4111	
4112	Examples:
4113	
4114	```text
4115	read_file
4116	write_file
4117	run_command
4118	search_web
4119	parse_pdf
4120	analyze_spreadsheet
4121	create_artifact
4122	git_diff
4123	```
4124	
4125	Each tool has a runtime-owned definition:
4126	
4127	```text
4128	tool_id
4129	version
4130	input_schema
4131	output_schema
4132	capabilities
4133	risk_class
4134	timeout
4135	resource_limits
4136	redaction_policy
4137	approval_policy
4138	```
4139	
4140	Experts only propose tool calls. The runtime validates and executes them.
4141	
4142	### Connector
4143	
4144	A connector provides authenticated access to an external service.
4145	
4146	Examples:
4147	
4148	```text
4149	GitHub
4150	Google Drive
4151	Dropbox
4152	Notion
4153	Google Calendar
4154	University LMS
4155	```
4156	
4157	A connector contains:
4158	
4159	- authentication configuration
4160	- supported actions
4161	- tool bindings
4162	- permission scopes
4163	- connection status
4164	- rate limits
4165	- redaction rules
4166	- account/workspace visibility
4167	
4168	An Operation may recommend a connector, but it cannot automatically authorize or install one.
4169	
4170	### Pack references
4171	
4172	A pack should reference capabilities rather than duplicate implementations:
4173	
4174	```text
4175	Student Pack
4176	├── Skill: Literature Review
4177	├── Tool: PDF Parser
4178	└── Connector recommendation: Google Drive
4179	```
4180	
4181	The actual PDF parser and Google Drive connector remain platform-owned or separately installed capabilities.
4182	
4183	This follows the useful extensibility ideas from Agent Zero and Dify while keeping execution and authorization outside agent-pack configuration.
4184	
4185	Do you accept this strict separation of **skills as knowledge, tools as executable capabilities, and connectors as authenticated integrations**?
4186

4205	The important refinement is to separate reusable definitions from Operation-specific assignments.
4206	
4207	## Recommended canonical model
4208	
4209	```text
4210	Pack
4211	├── Agent catalog
4212	│   ├── Researcher
4213	│   ├── Citation Manager
4214	│   ├── Programming Tutor
4215	│   └── Data Analyst
4216	│
4217	└── Operations
4218	    ├── Research Paper
4219	    │   ├── Researcher
4220	    │   ├── Citation Manager
4221	    │   └── Writing Coach
4222	    │
4223	    ├── Exam Preparation
4224	    │   ├── Study Planner
4225	    │   ├── Subject Tutor
4226	    │   └── Source Verifier
4227	    │
4228	    └── Programming Assignment
4229	        ├── Programming Tutor
4230	        ├── Developer
4231	        └── Debugger
4232	```
4233	
4234	Each reusable agent definition owns its default capabilities:
4235	
4236	```text
4237	Agent: Researcher
4238	├── Skills
4239	│   ├── Web Research
4240	│   ├── Literature Review
4241	│   └── Evidence Synthesis
4242	├── Tools
4243	│   ├── Web Search
4244	│   ├── Page Reader
4245	│   ├── PDF Parser
4246	│   └── Artifact Writer
4247	└── Connectors
4248	    ├── Google Drive
4249	    └── Reference Manager
4250	```
4251	
4252	An Operation then selects agents and may narrow or customize their capabilities:
4253	
4254	```text
4255	Operation: Research Paper
4256	└── Researcher assignment
4257	    ├── enabled skills
4258	    │   ├── Literature Review
4259	    │   └── Evidence Synthesis
4260	    ├── enabled tools
4261	    │   ├── Web Search
4262	    │   └── PDF Parser
4263	    ├── allowed connectors
4264	    │   └── Google Drive
4265	    └── operation instructions
4266	        └── Focus on academic sources
4267	```
4268	
4269	## Why the distinction matters
4270	
4271	The same Researcher can participate in multiple Operations without duplicating its entire profile:
4272	
4273	```text
4274	Researcher
4275	├── Research Paper
4276	├── Data Analysis
4277	├── Presentation Preparation
4278	└── Exam Preparation
4279	```
4280	
4281	Each Operation can narrow the Researcher’s behavior without creating a new copy.
4282	
4283	This gives us:
4284	
4285	- one source of truth for each agent
4286	- reusable agents across Operations
4287	- Operation-specific capability restrictions
4288	- fewer duplicated prompts
4289	- simpler pack upgrades
4290	- easier testing
4291	- less configuration drift
4292	- clearer behavior for coding agents maintaining the repository
4293	
4294	## Capability rule
4295	
4296	The effective capabilities of an agent in an Operation should be the intersection of several layers:
4297	
4298	```text
4299	Platform capability
4300	∩ Pack allowance
4301	∩ Agent defaults
4302	∩ Operation assignment
4303	∩ Workspace policy
4304	∩ User grants
4305	∩ Current task requirements
4306	= Effective capability
4307	```
4308	
4309	No lower-level configuration may widen an upper-level restriction.
4310	
4311	For example:
4312	
4313	```text
4314	Researcher supports web search
4315	Pack allows web search
4316	Operation enables web search
4317	Workspace allows external reads
4318	User permits external reads
4319	Task requests research
4320	→ Web search allowed
4321	```
4322	
4323	But:
4324	
4325	```text
4326	Developer supports repository writes
4327	Operation disables repository writes
4328	User gives Developer general write access
4329	→ Still denied inside that Operation
4330	```
4331	
4332	The Operation’s constraints remain effective.
4333	
4334	## Suggested pack structure
4335	
4336	```text
4337	agent-packs/student/v1/
4338	├── pack.yaml
4339	├── agents/
4340	│   ├── researcher/
4341	│   │   ├── agent.yaml
4342	│   │   ├── prompts/
4343	│   │   ├── skills.yaml

**Question 1: Isolation boundary**

The PRD currently makes one user container the boundary for:

- conversations
- files and projects
- memories
- browser state
- provider keys
- repository credentials
- task state
- agent processes

The control plane stores only identity, container mapping, and minimal operational metadata.

Is this a hard invariant?

Choose one and explain any exception:

1. **Hard invariant:** all user data and secrets remain inside the user container; the control plane only routes, authenticates, monitors, and provisions.
2. **Prototype-only boundary:** use the container initially, but central PostgreSQL may later own conversations, tasks, memories, or provider configuration.
3. **Hybrid from the start:** central PostgreSQL owns selected durable records, while files, secrets, browser state, and runtime execution remain in the container.

This decision determines the tenancy model, database schema, backup strategy, WebSocket routing, recovery design, and most of the repository structure.

**Verdict:** Shera Bot is a thoughtful platform vision, but not yet a validated startup. For a solo founder, the current prototype is far too broad and infrastructure-heavy.

The key problems in [PRD.md](E:/PROJECTS/shera-bot/docs/PRD.md):

- No demonstrated demand, payment commitment, or observed user workflow.
- Researchers, developers, and students are three different markets.
- “General-purpose AI assistant” provides no focused buying reason.
- The prototype includes production-grade security, recovery, tenancy, browser automation, provider support, and operations before validating one job.
- Multi-agent behavior is presented as the value, but trust, containment, and reliable unattended execution may be the actual opportunity.

My recommended wedge is:

> A local coding task runner for technical founders at 2–10 person TypeScript companies that fixes bounded test, lint, or typecheck failures and returns a reviewable branch with reproducible evidence.

The first pilot should be deliberately constrained:

- One GitHub repository
- TypeScript, pnpm, and Docker
- One agent and one task
- 15–90 minute unattended runs
- No deployment, package installation, browser tools, or Git remote writes
- Runtime-only BYOK model access
- Four-week founder-supported pilot at `$250/company`

The seven-day assignment is to interview five suitable technical founders, observe three real repository workflows, and ask two for a signed or deposit-backed `$250` pilot. Do not build the dashboard or three-agent platform until this produces repeat usage and payment.

1	# Design: Shera Bot as a Solo-Founder Startup
2	
3	Generated by /office-hours on 2026-09-08
4	Branch: unknown (the workspace is not currently a Git checkout)
5	Repo: E:/PROJECTS/shera-bot
6	Source: E:/PROJECTS/shera-bot/docs/PRD.md
7	Status: DRAFT_WITH_CONCERNS
8	Mode: Startup
9	
10	## Problem Statement
11	
12	The PRD describes Shera Bot as a private, general-purpose AI operating environment: three role-specific agents, persistent chats and workspaces, browser and terminal activity, multi-agent delegation, autonomous tasks, provider choice, local-first credentials and memory, approval gates, isolated user containers, recovery, backups, Git worktrees, and an admin console.
13	
14	That is a coherent platform vision. It is not yet a startup problem statement. The PRD describes what the system can do, but not a narrowly defined person who is currently losing time, money, or opportunity because a specific workflow is broken. It also does not contain evidence that anyone would pay for this product or be upset if it disappeared.
15	
16	My current diagnosis: **do not build the platform as specified as the first company product**. Treat it as a capability map and validate one painful, repeatable workflow with one persona. The first product should be a wedge that can deliver value without OAuth, three agents, custom providers, multi-tenant containers, autonomous triggers, browser control, backups, or an admin console.
17	
18	## Demand Evidence
19	
20	The PRD contains no direct demand evidence. Specifically, it does not identify:
21	
22	- a named user who has asked for this;
23	- an observed workflow and its current cost;
24	- a customer who has paid, committed budget, or requested a delivery date;
25	- a user who would need to scramble if the product disappeared;
26	- a measured outcome such as hours saved, tasks completed, or errors avoided.
27	
28	The five-user cap is a prototype constraint, not evidence of a five-user market. The audience order of researchers, individual developers, and students is a hypothesis, not segmentation validated by behavior. The phrase “general-purpose AI assistant” expands the market description while making the buying problem less precise.
29	
30	**Position:** the idea is currently a technically ambitious product hypothesis, not a validated startup. Evidence that would change this judgment is three independent users in one segment completing the same workflow repeatedly, at least one paying or making a credible budget commitment, and at least one user reporting that the old workflow is unacceptable after using the prototype.
31	
32	## Status Quo
33	
34	The PRD does not document what a target user does today. That is the largest missing section.
35	
36	Likely status-quo substitutes differ by segment:
37	
38	- Researchers: ChatGPT/Claude projects, Google Scholar, reference managers, notebooks, browser tabs, scripts, spreadsheets, and manual citation checking.
39	- Developers: an IDE assistant, terminal, GitHub issues, CI, separate browser tabs, local scripts, and human review of patches.
40	- Students: general chat assistants, uploaded documents, search engines, note-taking tools, and manual study workflows.
41	
42	The real competitor is not another multi-agent platform. It is the existing pile of tools plus a user who has learned how to operate it. Shera Bot only wins if it removes a painful coordination cost that the pile creates and does so reliably enough that users change their habit.
43	
44	The PRD currently assumes that a richer integrated environment is inherently better. That premise is unproven. Users may prefer a single excellent workflow inside tools they already trust over a new environment that asks them to migrate chats, files, credentials, projects, memory, and habits.
45	
46	## Target User & Narrowest Wedge
47	
48	The current target is too broad: researchers, developers, and students, with a general-purpose assistant underneath. A solo founder should select one buyer, one recurring job, and one measurable result.
49	
50	### Recommended wedge: long-running, approval-gated coding tasks for small software teams
51	
52	Initial persona: a technical founder at a 2–10 person TypeScript SaaS company using GitHub and pnpm who regularly delays small, well-specified fixes because running, validating, and reviewing them interrupts higher-priority work.
53	
54	Initial job: “Take one failing test, lint error, or typecheck error from issue to reviewable branch during a 15–90 minute unattended window, pause before actions outside the agreed sandbox, and leave a reproducible evidence trail when I return.”
55	
56	Smallest paid version: a local-only Docker runner for one GitHub repository and one pnpm/TypeScript task. The container mounts only a temporary worktree read/write and a generated task configuration read-only; it does not mount the Docker socket, home directory, SSH directory, cloud credentials, or arbitrary host paths. It runs as a non-root user with dropped Linux capabilities, a process/time/memory limit, and no inbound network. Outbound network is denied except HTTPS to one user-selected model endpoint through a host-side proxy. The user supplies that provider key at runtime; it is never written into the worktree or activity log. The runner may read/write inside the mounted worktree and run Git plus configured pnpm validation commands, then returns a reviewable patch and activity log. Package installation, Git remote writes, deployment, access outside the worktree, and other secret use are disabled in the pilot. One agent is sufficient. A Telegram-like dashboard, browser/research tools, group chat, autonomous schedules, custom provider marketplace, multi-session recovery, and three-agent identity system are not required to test this job.
57	
58	The pilot has no remote approval dashboard. Because the allowed task surface is pre-approved and external writes are disabled, any action outside the allowlist stops the task and writes a pending-action record. The user reviews that record when they return and starts a new run with an expanded allowlist if desired. “While away” therefore means unattended execution only inside the pre-approved boundary, never indefinite live authorization.
59	
60	The minimum evidence trail contains the task request, start/end time, commands with exit codes, files changed, diff summary, validation results, approval decisions, failure reason if incomplete, and the exact commit/branch or patch the user can review.
61	
62	Why this is the best starting hypothesis for a solo founder:
63	
64	- The pain can be measured in completed tasks, elapsed time, reviewable output, and fewer context switches.
65	- The buyer has a clearer ability to pay than students and many individual researchers.
66	- The workflow has a natural trust boundary: code changes and external writes require approval.
67	- The PRD already contains useful product primitives for this wedge: worktrees, validation, task state, boundary approvals, restart recovery as a future option, and redaction.
68	- A user can try it on one repository without uploading source code to a new hosted platform or adopting a whole personal operating system.
69	
70	This is still a hypothesis. If interviews show that researchers have more urgent, repeated pain and will pay, switch the wedge rather than defending the architecture.
71	
72	## The Six Forcing Questions
73	
74	### Q1: Demand Reality
75	
76	Unknown. The PRD supplies no user behavior, payment, or “do not disappear” evidence. Before building the platform, get five interviews and three observed sessions in one segment. Do not count compliments, waitlists, or generic AI enthusiasm.
77	
78	### Q2: Status Quo
79	
80	Unknown. Ask each candidate to screen-share the last real task they solved with their current tools. Record every handoff between chat, editor, terminal, browser, Git, and notes. Quantify time, repeated work, blocked steps, and the point at which they abandon or defer the task.
81	
82	### Q3: Desperate Specificity
83	
84	Unknown. “Researchers,” “developers,” and “students” are categories, not customers. Name an actual person, their role, the recurring task, and the consequence of leaving it unsolved. For the recommended wedge, that might be a technical founder who loses half a day each week to context recovery and cautious execution of repository changes; it must be a real person, not an invented persona.
85	
86	### Q4: Narrowest Wedge
87	
88	The PRD is several products at once. The narrowest credible paid wedge is one repository, one task, one agent, one isolated worktree, bounded tool use, stop-on-boundary behavior, and a reviewable result. If a user will not pay for that, adding group chat and more infrastructure will not fix the problem.
89	
90	### Q5: Observation & Surprise
91	
92	Unknown. No observed user sessions are reported. A demo or survey will not answer this. Watch a user work without helping. The important discovery is likely to be what they do instead of using a new agent: copy prompts between tools, avoid risky automation, keep a manual checklist, or abandon tasks that cross too many context boundaries.
93	
94	### Q6: Future-Fit
95	
96	Potentially positive, but not yet a thesis. Better models may increase the value of reliable task execution, approvals, recovery, and auditability. They may also make generic multi-agent interfaces commoditized. The durable claim cannot be “AI gets better.” It must be: “As models become capable enough to act, small teams will pay for a trusted execution layer that lets them delegate long-running repository work without surrendering control.” That claim needs user behavior and retention evidence.
97	
98	## Constraints
99	
100	- Solo-founder bandwidth is the binding constraint; the current scope spans product, distributed runtime, security, browser automation, model adapters, data infrastructure, and operations.
101	- Trust and safety are part of the product, not polish. A single credential leak, cross-user isolation failure, or unsafe external action can end the company.
102	- Model providers, pricing, and tool behavior will change faster than a large platform can be stabilized.
103	- The PRD says “private prototype,” but many requirements are production-grade: durable replay, backups, restart-safe effects, tenancy isolation, custom providers, recovery semantics, and cross-browser quality.
104	- No real billing, export, purge, enterprise controls, or public launch are included, so the prototype cannot yet prove willingness to pay or operational viability.
105	- The product must remain independent from Grok/xAI branding and implementation, as already stated in the PRD.
106	
107	## Premises
108	
109	1. **A multi-agent environment is more valuable than a single excellent workflow.** This is currently unproven. The first user test should compare the narrow single-agent task runner against the full conceptual environment.
110	2. **Users want a new workspace rather than integration into their existing tools.** This is risky. Measure willingness to connect a repository and use a new execution surface before building migration-heavy features.
111	3. **Researchers, developers, and students share enough pain for one product.** They do not share a buyer, urgency, workflow, or budget by default. Pick one segment until evidence supports expansion.
112	4. **Local-first isolation is a customer-visible advantage.** It may be a strong trust feature, but only if users understand it and choose it over simpler hosted alternatives.
113	5. **Autonomy is the primary value.** It may instead be approval-aware reliability, containment, and evidence of what happened. Test the benefit users actually pay for.
114	6. **The architecture should be built before the wedge is known.** This is backwards for a solo founder. Architecture should follow the first paid workflow, with explicit seams only where the workflow proves them necessary.
115	
116	## Approaches Considered
117	
118	### Approach A: Full Shera platform from the PRD
119	
120	Summary: Build the three-agent, multi-tenant, containerized assistant platform with all prototype acceptance criteria.
121	
122	Effort: XL
123	
124	Risk: High
125	
126	Pros:
127	
128	- Strong long-term capability map and room for differentiated trust/recovery behavior.
129	- Exercises the complete runtime architecture early.
130	- Could support multiple future wedges if the core interaction proves valuable.
131	
132	Cons:
133	
134	- No demand proof before a very large build.
135	- Security, recovery, provider adapters, browser state, and real-time semantics create multiple failure surfaces.
136	- Makes it hard to learn which feature caused value or failure.
137	- A solo founder can spend months building infrastructure without discovering a paying user.
138	
139	Reuses: all sections of the current PRD.
140	
141	### Approach B: Single-agent coding task runner (recommended)
142	
143	Summary: Build one bounded local workflow for one repository task: a restricted Docker mount, an isolated worktree, allowlisted commands, validation, stop-on-boundary behavior, and reviewable output. Cross-session resumability is a post-validation option.
144	
145	Effort: M
146	
147	Risk: Medium
148	
149	Pros:
150	
151	- Clear user, job, result, and willingness-to-pay test.
152	- Reuses the PRD's strongest primitives without requiring a platform shell.
153	- Creates a meaningful trust advantage if containment and evidence are excellent.
154	- Can be tested with five users in weeks rather than a full platform cycle.
155	
156	Cons:
157	
158	- Developer tooling is crowded and incumbents are strong.
159	- Requires careful sandboxing and secret handling from day one.
160	- May reveal that the strongest value is an integration or service, not a standalone workspace.
161	
162	Reuses: Git worktrees, validation runner, task state, boundary-stop records, redaction, and provider abstraction where needed. Recovery checkpoints are explicitly deferred.
163	
164	### Approach C: Research evidence pipeline
165	
166	Summary: For one research persona, turn a question plus a bounded document/web corpus into an evidence table, citations, unresolved questions, and a report.
167	
168	Effort: M
169	
170	Risk: Medium
171	
172	Pros:
173	
174	- The Researcher role has a concrete output and quality bar.
175	- Evidence tables and citations are more specific than a generic chat assistant.
176	- Can start as a service or concierge workflow before building a platform.
177	
178	Cons:
179	
180	- Researchers and students may have low willingness to pay.
181	- Citation accuracy and source freshness are difficult to guarantee.
182	- Existing research and chat products make differentiation and distribution difficult.
183	
184	Reuses: document/PDF parsing, web reading, citations, source indexes, memory, artifact generation.
185	
186	## Recommended Approach
187	
188	Choose **Approach B** for validation, while keeping the broader PRD as a non-binding north star.
189	
190	The first product should not be called a general-purpose assistant in its positioning. Position it around a result: “A local coding task runner that fixes bounded TypeScript validation failures while you are away and returns a reviewable branch with reproducible evidence.” Do not build three agents until users repeatedly ask for delegation across distinct roles or the single agent demonstrably hits a workflow boundary.
191	
192	The product decision should be reversible. After the first validation cycle, continue only if users complete tasks repeatedly, accept the approval model, and either pay or make a credible budget commitment. Otherwise, pivot the wedge or stop; do not use the existing architecture as a reason to continue.
193	
194	## Open Questions
195	
196	- Which actual user will be the first design partner, and what repository task will they delegate?
197	- What is the current status quo for that user, measured in time and handoffs?
198	- Will users trust a new runner with repository credentials and local environment setup?
199	- Does the technical founder use the product personally, or does an engineering manager become the economic buyer after the pilot?
200	- Which execution boundary matters most: code mutation, network writes, deployments, or secrets?
201	- What is the minimum outcome that justifies payment: completed tasks, reduced cycle time, or fewer interruptions?
202	- Will the local Docker requirement be acceptable to the first five technical founders, or does onboarding friction destroy the wedge?
203	- What is the distribution channel: direct outreach to technical founders, developer communities, GitHub integration, or an agency/consulting wedge?
204	- Can the solo founder support incidents involving credentials, sandbox escape, duplicated external effects, and recovery?
205	- What evidence would justify expanding from one agent to three and from one repository to multi-project tenancy?
206	
207	## Success Criteria
208	
209	### Validation gate before platform expansion
210	
211	- 10 conversations with people who recently performed the target workflow.
212	- 5 observed sessions using their real tools and repository, without a guided demo.
213	- 3 design partners complete the same task type twice.
214	- At least 2 users ask to use it again without prompting.
215	- At least 1 user pays, signs a paid pilot, or commits a specific budget and start date.
216	- A credible budget commitment means either a non-refundable deposit or a signed pilot agreement naming price, start date, and repository task class; verbal enthusiasm does not count.
217	- The runner produces a reviewable branch and the defined evidence trail without exposing credentials or accessing files outside the agreed worktree.
218	- Approval, cancellation, and bounded retry behavior are understood by users and recover safely in test scenarios. Multi-session crash recovery is deferred unless interviews identify it as paid pain.
219	
220	### Product signal that supports expansion
221	
222	- Users describe the value in their own words as delegation/reliability/trust, not “a cool AI workspace.”
223	- Users return for the same job at least weekly.
224	- Retention persists even when model quality improves or changes provider.
225	- Users request additional roles or agents because the current workflow has a proven boundary, not because the platform menu suggests it.
226	
227	### Continue, pivot, or stop rubric
228	
229	- **Continue:** at least 3 design partners each run 2 qualifying tasks, at least 2 ask to reuse it, and at least 1 pays the pilot price.
230	- **Extend the test once:** exactly 2 design partners complete a second task and at least 1 signs the paid pilot; recruit 5 more candidates without expanding product scope, then apply the rubric again.
231	- **Pivot the wedge:** observed pain is real but users consistently want a different recurring task, deployment mode, or buyer; preserve only primitives required by that newly observed job.
232	- **Stop:** fewer than 3 of 10 interviewees performed a comparable workflow in the last month, fewer than 2 complete a second task, nobody accepts a concrete paid pilot, or users refuse the local trust/onboarding model.
233	- **Stop for feasibility:** the constrained runner cannot prevent access outside the worktree, produces unreproducible changes, or total model/tool cost for a company's pilot exceeds 20% of that company's US$250 pilot revenue before human support time.
234	
235	## Distribution Plan
236	
237	Start with founder-led distribution. Recruit technical founders at 2–10 person TypeScript SaaS companies through direct outreach, local developer communities, and personal network referrals. Ask for a real failing test, lint error, or typecheck error, not a product demo.
238	
239	The first offer is a four-week, founder-supported pilot at **US$250 per company**, covering up to 10 bounded tasks on one repository. The price is a demand probe, not final packaging. Track model/tool cost per task and keep it below 20% of collected revenue; track human support separately because a pilot that requires constant founder intervention has not validated software economics.
240	
241	Users receive the pilot as a versioned local Docker image plus a small configuration file for one GitHub repository and the allowed pnpm commands. The founder onboards each company live, supplies updates manually, and maintains a signed checksum and changelog for every pilot release. No public package, auto-update system, cloud dashboard, or GitHub App is required for the first five users. If the pilot validates, publish the runner through GitHub Container Registry and add CI that builds, tests, scans, signs, and publishes versioned images from tagged releases.
242	
243	Do not launch open signup, student pricing, a broad provider marketplace, or a public multi-agent platform before the first wedge has repeat usage. The initial distribution advantage must come from learning faster than larger competitors, not from trying to out-feature them.
244	
245	## Dependencies
246	
247	- A real design partner and access to a non-sensitive or safely redacted repository.
248	- A clear threat model and sandbox boundary before handling credentials or external writes.
249	- A minimal local execution runtime with single-task state and a worktree; durable cross-session orchestration is deferred.
250	- A host-side HTTPS model proxy that injects a runtime-only BYOK credential, restricts the destination to the configured provider, meters usage, and redacts request/response metadata stored in the activity log.
251	- A reliable validation runner for the target language/build system.
252	- A way to capture task evidence without exposing secrets.
253	- A pricing and pilot agreement that tests payment rather than compliments.
254	
255	## The Assignment
256	
257	Within the next seven days, recruit five technical founders at 2–10 person TypeScript SaaS companies who recently delayed or manually shepherded a failing test, lint error, or typecheck error across chat, editor, terminal, and Git. Observe three of them doing the task with their existing tools. Write down the exact task, every handoff, elapsed time, failure/recovery point, and what they would pay to delegate. Then ask two of them to sign or place a deposit on the US$250 four-week pilot.
258	
259	Do not build the dashboard or three agents during this assignment. The deliverable is a one-page workflow map, three observed sessions, and a yes/no payment signal.
260	
261	## What I noticed about how you think
262	
263	- You wrote unusually explicit safety boundaries: “risky actions pause for approval by default,” credentials never enter prompts or logs, and unsafe recovery must pause. That suggests trust and control may be a stronger product thesis than agent novelty.
264	- You separated prototype non-goals from future work and stated a decision boundary. That discipline is useful, but the current acceptance list still contains production-scale infrastructure that conflicts with the private-prototype label.
265	- You designed for restart recovery, missed-event replay, idempotent commands, worktrees, and redaction before proving a single user workflow. That is strong systems thinking; the founder risk is spending that strength before the market tells you which reliability property is valuable.
266	- The phrase “general-purpose AI assistant platform” and the audience list of researchers, developers, and students are the least specific parts of the PRD. Those should be treated as placeholders until observed user behavior replaces them.
267	
268	## Reviewer Concerns
269	
270	- This memo recommends a developer wedge without direct user evidence. That is a validation hypothesis, not a market conclusion.
271	- The local-only TypeScript/pnpm constraint is deliberately narrow. It improves feasibility and learning speed but may exclude the founder's easiest-to-reach design partners.
272	- The PRD may encode a different founder advantage or distribution channel that is not visible in the document. A founder with privileged access to researchers, for example, could rationally choose the research wedge despite lower default willingness to pay.
273	- No external landscape search was run. The assessment intentionally relies on the PRD and startup diagnostic principles; competitive claims should be verified after the first user observations.
274

---
name: grilling
description: Grill the user relentlessly about a plan, decision, or idea. Use when the user wants to stress-test their thinking, or uses any 'grill' trigger phrases.
---

Interview the user relentlessly until you reach a shared understanding. Map this as a **design tree**: every decision branches into the decisions that hang off it.

Work the tree in **rounds**. The **frontier** is every decision whose prerequisites are already settled: the questions you can ask _now_ without guessing at answers you haven't heard yet. Ask the whole frontier in one round: number each question and give your recommended answer. Then wait for the user's answers before the next round.

Each question should be formatted like so:

```
❓ **Q1** - **<question title>**: <question body, might be multiple paragraphs, including multiple choices>

➡️ <your recommended answer>
```

Each round the user answers reshapes the tree: settled decisions push the frontier outward and unblock questions that depended on them. Recompute the frontier and ask the next round. A question whose answer depends on another question still open in this round belongs to a _later_ round, not this one.

Finding _facts_ is your job, never the user's. When a frontier question needs a fact from the environment (filesystem, tools, etc.), dispatch a sub-agent to find it; don't ask the user for anything you could look up yourself. Don't block on it: a running exploration is an unsettled prerequisite, so only the questions downstream of it wait for the sub-agent to report; ask the rest of the frontier now. The _decisions_ are the user's: put each to them and wait.

The session is done when the frontier is empty: every branch of the design tree visited, nothing left silently assumed. Do not act on it until the user confirms you have reached a shared understanding.

=== grilling doc head ===
## What it does

`grilling` is the interview loop that stress-tests a plan, a decision, or an idea before anyone acts on it. It maps the subject as a **design tree** — every decision branches into the decisions that hang off it — and interviews you branch by branch until nothing is left silently assumed.

It does not ask one question at a time, and it does not ask everything at once. Each **round** asks the whole **frontier**: every decision whose prerequisites are already settled, and nothing else. Two questions never share a round if one depends on the other — a question that hinges on an answer still open belongs to a later round. Your answers settle decisions, the frontier moves outward, and the next round asks what that unblocked. Thirteen questions typically land in about three rounds rather than thirteen.

## When to reach for it

Type `/grilling`, or the [agent](https://www.aihero.dev/ai-coding-dictionary/agent) reaches for it on its own when a task fits. It is the only [skill](https://www.aihero.dev/ai-coding-dictionary/skill) in the grilling family that is model-invoked, which is why you rarely type it: usually a skill you *did* type is running it for you.

Typing `/grilling` directly gets you the plain interview and nothing else. Where you want something more than that:

| What you have | Reach for |
| --- | --- |
| You aren't working in a working directory | [grill-me](https://aihero.dev/skills-grill-me) — the same [session](https://www.aihero.dev/ai-coding-dictionary/session), under a name the agent will never fire by itself |
| You are in a working directory | [grill-with-docs](https://aihero.dev/skills-grill-with-docs) — the same session, and it writes `CONTEXT.md` and ADRs as it goes |
| An effort too big to hold in one session | [wayfinder](https://aihero.dev/skills-wayfinder) — it charts a map and runs grilling inside the decision tickets |
| A question that talking cannot settle — how something should look or feel | [prototype](https://aihero.dev/skills-prototype) — build the throwaway version, then come back |
| A skill of your own that needs an interview | Invoke `/grilling` from it, rather than writing another interview |

## The round, the frontier, and who decides

Three ideas carry the whole skill.

The **design tree** is the model of the subject: decisions with decisions hanging off them. The **frontier** is the set of decisions whose prerequisites are all settled — the only questions that can honestly be asked yet. A **round** is one frontier, asked in full and answered in full.

Inside a round every question arrives in a fixed shape: numbered and titled behind a `❓`, then the body, then the agent's recommended answer alone on a `➡️` line. That is what makes a round answerable by number — "1 yes, 2 the second option, 3 no, here's why" — instead of by quoting questions back. The format has one known rough edge: the recommendation sometimes argues *against* the question as it was worded, so agreeing with the recommendation means answering "no" to the question. When that happens, answer the recommendation and say so.

The other half of the design is the split between facts and decisions. Facts are the skill's own job: when a frontier question needs something the [environment](https://www.aihero.dev/ai-coding-dictionary/environment) can settle, it dispatches a [sub-agent](https://www.aihero.dev/ai-coding-dictionary/subagent) to go and find out rather than asking you. It does not block on that — only the questions downstream of a running exploration wait. Decisions are yours, and it must wait for them. An agent running `grilling` that answers its own decisions has broken the skill, not interpreted it liberally. The session ends when the frontier is empty, and it will not act on what you agreed until you confirm you have reached a shared understanding.

The honest limit: the frontier is the agent's judgement, not a computed graph. It can put two questions in one round and only afterwards discover that one answer should have changed the other. There is no guard against that beyond telling it, which reopens the affected branch in the next round.

## What lives here and what lives in the wrappers

This page covers the mechanism. The things people most often want are documented one level up.

| Question | Where it is answered |
| --- | --- |
| The tree, the frontier, rounds, the question format, facts vs decisions | Here |
| How long a session should run, what to do with a question you can't answer by talking, how to avoid nodding along | [grill-me](https://aihero.dev/skills-grill-me) |

❓ **Q40** — **Workspace model**: The session decided "one pinned pack per workspace" (D19) and "one account-level credit balance" (D21), but never defined what a *workspace* actually is or how many a user gets. This is now load-bearing: it decides the container/account/data-scoping model and whether the UI needs a workspace switcher.

What is a workspace in Shera, and how many can one user have?

1. **One workspace per user** — the container is the workspace. No switcher in the UI. Records are still workspace-scoped internally so multi-workspace can be enabled later without a migration.
2. **Multiple user-created workspaces in v1** — the user creates and switches between workspaces, each pinned to its own pack and context, all sharing the account-level credit balance.
3. **One workspace per pack** — a user gets one workspace per customer pack (e.g., a student workspace now, a developer workspace later) inside the same container.
4. **One workspace per project** — workspace is the project boundary; the user creates one per repository/course and each pins its own pack.

➡️ **Recommended: option 1** for the prototype. The PRD already assumes one container per user, and D21 puts credits at the account level, so a single implicit workspace keeps onboarding zero-config (D16) and the data model simple. Scoping records by `workspace_id` from day one preserves option 2 or 3 later without a migration — which is exactly the migration-readiness requirement from D2.

❓ **Q42** — **Operation coordinator**: The PRD's group chats include an *orchestrator* that routes work and may add/remove agents (§4.2), and D29 makes the General Assistant the default coordinator of pack-defined Operations. But D41 just legalized **ad-hoc** Operations with a custom roster — and the session never decided who coordinates when there's no pack definition, or whether the GA is even mandatory.

Who coordinates an Operation?

1. **GA always coordinates** — every Operation (pack-defined or ad-hoc) includes the General Assistant as coordinator; it cannot be removed in v1. Predictable, one synthesis voice, but an extra model participant in every Operation even when unneeded.
2. **GA is the default coordinator, but removable** — pack definitions may name a different coordinator expert (`coordinator: <expert>` or `none`); the user may remove the GA from any Operation. Without a coordinator, experts coordinate peer-to-peer through the typed protocol (D7); the runtime remains the safety authority regardless (D4).
3. **No coordinator concept** — all Operations are peer-to-peer; the GA joins only as an ordinary participant when explicitly added.

➡️ **Recommended: option 2.** It keeps the PRD's orchestrator behavior as the default (zero-config, matches D4's "General Assistant proposes, control plane validates"), gives ad-hoc Operations a sensible default without forcing one, and needs no new machinery — peer-to-peer coordination is just D7 messages without a synthesizer. Option 1's forced GA would waste credits on simple two-expert jobs; option 3 breaks the PRD's group-chat model and pushes orchestration decisions back onto the user.

❓ **Q44** — **Terminology**: The session left "expert" and "agent" used interchangeably. The PRD says "agents" throughout; your revised product model says "Experts." Both words will appear in the same repo — pack YAML, TypeScript identifiers, DB tables, UI copy — and a single rule is cheaper to adopt now than a rename later.

Which vocabulary goes where?

1. **"Expert" in the UI, `agent` in code** — UI copy and product docs say Experts and Operations; TypeScript identifiers, Zod schemas, DB tables, and pack YAML use `agent`, `operation`, `pack` (e.g., `agents/researcher/agent.yaml`). Keeps the PRD and agent-framework terminology intact while the product voice stays yours.
2. **"Expert" everywhere** — one vocabulary end to end (`experts/researcher/expert.yaml`, `expert_id` columns). Consistent, but diverges from the PRD text and from how Pi/agent tooling names things, so every upstream-facing mapping becomes a translation.
3. **"Agent" everywhere** — matches the PRD exactly, but drops the Expert/Operation product framing you deliberately built in D29–D43.

➡️ **Recommended: option 1.** The UI/product layer is where "Expert" carries meaning for your audience; the code layer is where `agent` matches the PRD, the Pi adapter, and every agent-framework convention you'll borrow. One documented mapping ("Expert = user-facing term for an agent") in the README prevents drift. Option 2 makes you fight upstream naming forever; option 3 quietly erases the product model you just spent 40 questions building.
